.
Both figures are half-space stationary two-parameter log-gamma polymer model with weights described in Definition
4.4
. The equality in distribution is justified by Lemma
4.2
.

Figure 5
.
This model connects the product stationary case and the two-parameter stationary case. Let
\gamma
denote the weight at
(2,1)
. If
\gamma=1,
i.e., a constant weight
1
, then this model becomes the two-parameter stationary model. If
\gamma=\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha-t)
and
\beta=\alpha
, then this model becomes the product stationary model.

Definition 4.3
.

Consider the half-space geometry
\mathcal{D}
. We define the following weights:

(4.2)

\omega_{i,j}=\begin{cases}1,&\textrm{if }i=j=1,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\alpha+t\right),&\textrm{if }i=j\geq 2,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\alpha-t\right),&\textrm{if }j=1,i\geq 2,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}(2\alpha),&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Let
Z^{t}(N,M)
denote the partition function from
(1,1)
to
(N,M)
. We refer to this as the half-space log-gamma polymer model with product stationary initial condition (Figure
4
).

One can easily check that

(4.3)

Z^{t}(1+\cdot,1)/Z^{t}(1,1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\mathcal{T}_{-t,t}(\cdot)

where
\mathcal{T}_{-t,t}
is defined in (
1.3
). Moreover,
Z^{t}(N,M)
satisfies the same recurrence relations as in (
1.1
). Hence,
Z^{t}(N,N)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\mathcal{Z}^{t}(N,N)
in Definition
1.5
.

Definition 4.4
.

Consider the half-space geometry
\mathcal{D}
. We define the following weights:

(4.4)

\omega_{i,j}=\begin{cases}1,&\textrm{if }i=j=1,\\
1,&\textrm{if }i=2,j=1,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\beta+t\right),&\textrm{if }i=j=2,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\alpha+t\right),&\textrm{if }i>j=2,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\alpha+\beta\right),&\textrm{if }i=j\geq 3,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\alpha-t\right),&\textrm{if }j=1,i\geq 3,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}(2\alpha),&\textrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

where
\alpha>0,

t\in(0,\alpha)
,
\beta\in(-t,\infty)
. Let
Z^{t,\beta}(N,M)
denote the partition function from
(1,1)
to
(N,M)
. We refer to this as the half-space log-gamma polymer model with two-parameter stationary initial condition (Figure
3
).

One can also check that

(4.5)

Z^{t,\beta}(2+\cdot,2)/Z^{t,\beta}(2,2)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\mathcal{T}_{\beta,t}(\cdot)

where
\mathcal{T}_{\beta,t}
is defined in (
1.3
).
The fact that the first two rows encode the two-parameter stationary initial condition results in an index shift from
Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)
to
\mathcal{Z}^{t,\beta}(N-1,N-1)
which is introduced in Definition
1.9
.

Definition 4.5
.

Consider the half-space geometry
\mathcal{D}
. We define the following weights:

(4.6)

\omega_{i,j}=\begin{cases}1,&\textrm{if }i=j=1,\\
\gamma,&\textrm{if }i=2,j=1,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\beta+t\right),&\textrm{if }i=j=2,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\beta+\alpha\right),&\textrm{if }i>j=2,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\alpha+t\right),&\textrm{if }i=j\geq 3,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\alpha-t\right),&\textrm{if }j=1,i\geq 3,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}(2\alpha),&\textrm{otherwise},\end{cases}

where
\alpha>0,

t\in(0,\alpha)
,
\beta\in(-t,\infty)
. Let
\mathcal{G}^{t,\beta}(N,M)
denote the partition function from
(1,1)
to
(N,M)
under the above model. This model is shown in Figure
5
.

If
\gamma=1
, then by Lemma
4.2
,
\mathcal{G}^{t,\beta}(N,N)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)
for all
N\geq 1
. If instead,
\gamma=\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\alpha-t\right)
and
\beta=\alpha
, then
\mathcal{G}^{t,\beta}(N,N)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}Z^{t}(N,N)
for all
N\geq 1
.

By
[
5
, Remark 1.7]
, it is known that if
\beta=t
,
\mathcal{T}_{\beta,t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\mathcal{T}_{-t,t}
, i.e., the product stationary measure. In terms of half-space log-gamma polymer model, we see that
Z^{t,t}(2+N,2)/Z^{t,t}(2,2)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\mathcal{T}_{-t,t}
. However, we make it clear that
Z^{t,t}(N,N)
and
\mathcal{Z}^{t}(N,N)
are not equal in distribution due to the fact that the weight at
(2,2)
contributes non-trivially to
Z^{t,t}(N,N)
(even in the limit) while
\mathcal{Z}^{t}(N,N)
has no such issue as the weight at
(1,1)
is just
1
. Therefore, it is better to only compute the distribution of
Z^{t}(N,N)
. On the other hand, when
\beta=-t,
the model
Z^{t,\beta}
is not defined because of the random variable
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\beta+t)
at
(2,2)
. As a result, we will only analyze distributions of
Z^{t,\beta}
when
\beta\neq t,-t
.
To preview the analysis,
Z^{t,\beta}
with
\beta>t
and
Z^{t}
will be analyzed together. The regime
-t<\beta<t
for
Z^{t,\beta}
, however, is handled separately and requires a different method.

To obtain the distribution of
Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)
, we start with the half-space log-gamma polymer model with the introduction of a new first-row parameter
u
.

Definition 4.6
.

Consider the half-space geometry
\mathcal{D}
. We define the following weights:

(4.7)

\omega_{i,j}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\beta+t\right),&\textrm{if }i=j=2,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\beta+u\right),&\textrm{if }i=2,j=1,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\beta+\alpha\right),&\textrm{if }i>j=2,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\alpha+t\right),&\textrm{if }i=j\geq 3,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(\alpha+u\right),&\textrm{if }j=1,i\geq 3,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}\left(u+t\right),&\textrm{if }i=j=1,\\
\mathrm{Gamma}^{-1}(2\alpha),&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Let
Z^{u}_{\beta>t}(N,M)
(
Z^{u}_{\beta<t}(N,M)
) denote the partition function from
(1,1)
to
(N,M)
under the above model when
\beta>t
(respectively,
\beta<t
). This is shown in Figure
6
.

5.
Pfaffian Structure of the half-space log-gamma model

Our goal is to
identify the exact distribution of the polymer free energy under the stationary
initial condition. The following theorem, established in
[
17
, Theorem 5.10]
, gives a
Fredholm Pfaffian expression for the Laplace transform of the partition function. We adapt it to our model (
4.7
).
This representation is the starting point for our analysis.

u+t
(1,1)
\beta+u
Z^{u}_{\beta>t}(N,N)
\alpha+t
\alpha+t
\alpha+t
\alpha+t
\beta+t
\alpha+u
\alpha+\beta
2\alpha
2\alpha
2\alpha

Figure 6
.
log-gamma polymer model as described in
(
4.7
)
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Fix
N\in\Z_{\geq 3}.
Let
t\in(\frac{1}{N},\frac{1}{2}),

\alpha>t
,
\beta>\frac{1}{N}
and
t>u>\frac{1}{N}.

Consider the half-space log-gamma polymer partition function

Z(N,N)

with boundary parameter
\beta
, first row parameter
u
, second row parameter
t
, and bulk parameters
\alpha
.
Choose a real number
d>0
satisfying

(5.1)

\frac{1}{N}<d<\min\{1/2,u,\beta,t,\alpha\}.

Then for every
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
one has the identity

(5.2)

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-\tau+\log Z(N,N)}}\right]=\Pf(J-{\mathbb{\check{K}}})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\tau,\infty)},

where
\mathbb{\check{K}}
is a
2\times 2
matrix kernel

(5.3)

\mathbb{\check{K}}(X,Y)=\begin{pmatrix}\check{K}(X,Y)&-\partial_{Y}\check{K}(X,Y)\\
-\partial_{X}\check{K}(X,Y)&\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\check{K}(X,Y)\end{pmatrix}.

The scalar function
\check{K}
is given by the double contour integral

(5.4)

\check{K}(X,Y)=\int\limits_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dZ}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dW}{2\pi i}\;e^{XZ+YW}\;\frac{\Gamma(u+Z)}{\Gamma(u-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(u+W)}{\Gamma(u-W)}\frac{\Gamma(t+Z)}{\Gamma(t-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(t+W)}{\Gamma(t-W)}G_{\alpha,\beta}(Z)\,G_{\alpha,\beta}(W)\,Q(Z,W),

with

(5.5)

G_{\alpha,\beta}(Z)=\left(\frac{\Gamma(\beta+Z)}{\Gamma(\beta-Z)}\right)\left(\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+Z)}{\Gamma(\alpha-Z)}\right)^{N-2},\quad Q(Z,W)=\Gamma(-2Z)\Gamma(-2W)\,\frac{\sin\bigl(\pi(Z-W)\bigr)}{\sin\bigl(\pi(Z+W)\bigr)}.

6.
Reformulation of kernel for High density phase when
\beta>t

To extract the exact distribution of the half-space log-gamma free energy under the stationary initial conditions, we need to remove the effect of the random variable at
(1,1)
and then take the limit of
u\rightarrow-t
.

6.1.
Removing the weight at
(1,1)

Recall that
\omega_{1,1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\text{Gamma}^{-1}(u+t)
. For
\beta>t
, we let
Z^{u,-}_{\beta>t}
be the partition function with the weight at
(1,1)
being replaced by a constant
1
. Similarly, we define
Z^{u,-}_{\beta<t}
for
\beta<t
. Since every polymer path from
(1,1)
to
(N,N)
must go through
(1,1)
, it follows that

(6.1)

Z^{u}_{\beta>t}=\omega_{1,1}Z^{u,-}_{\beta>t},\quad Z^{u}_{\beta<t}=\omega_{1,1}Z^{u,-}_{\beta<t}.

For any
x>0,
we rewrite the Laplace transform of
Z^{u}_{\beta>t}(N,N)
as

(6.2)

\mathbbm{E}[e^{-xZ^{u}_{\beta>t}(N,N)}]=\mathbbm{E}[e^{-xZ^{u,-}_{\beta>t}(N,N)\omega_{1,1}}].

Given the independence of
\omega_{1,1}
and
Z^{u,-}_{\beta>t}(N,N)
, we first integrate
\omega_{1,1}
to get:

(6.3)

\Gamma(u+t)\mathbbm{E}[e^{-xZ^{u,-}_{\beta>t}(N,N)\omega_{1,1}}]=\mathbbm{E}\bigg[2(xZ_{\beta>t}^{u,-}(N,N))^{\frac{u+t}{2}}K_{-u-t}\left(2\sqrt{xZ_{\beta>t}^{u,-}(N,N)}\right)\bigg]

where
K_{-\nu}
is the modified Bessel function
1
defined to be

(6.4)

2c^{\nu/2}K_{-\nu}(2\sqrt{c})=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}e^{-cy-y^{-1}}y^{-\nu-1}dy\quad\text{ for }c>0.

Remark 6.1
.

Theorem
5.1
provides a Fredholm Pfaffian representation for the LHS of (
6.3
). When we take the
u\rightarrow-t
limit, the factor
\Gamma(u+t)
on the RHS of (
6.3
) diverges to infinity, while the expectation on the RHS of (
6.3
) admits a finite and nontrivial limit from which the exact distribution of the stationary half-space log-gamma free energy can be extracted. Consequently, the Fredholm Pfaffian on the LHS of (
6.3
) must vanish at a compensating rate so that its product with
\Gamma(u+t)
converges to a well-defined limit. This observation motivates the kernel decomposition introduced below.

6.2.
Kernel Decomposition

Recall the definition of
G_{\alpha,\beta}
in (
5.5
).
We define the following functions for further decomposition of the kernel.

(6.5)

\displaystyle A(X):=e^{-Xu}\frac{\Gamma(t-u)}{\Gamma(t+u)}G_{\alpha,\beta}(-u),\quad B(Y):=\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}e^{YW}\frac{\Gamma(t+W)}{\Gamma(t-W)}G_{\alpha,\beta}(W)\Gamma(-2W)\frac{\Gamma(1-u+W)}{\Gamma(1-u-W)}.

Lemma 6.2
.

For any
N\in\Z_{\geq 3},

t\in(\frac{1}{N},\frac{1}{2}),

t>u>\frac{1}{N}
, and
\alpha,\beta>t
,
we have the following decomposition of kernel:

(6.6)

{\mathbb{\check{K}}}(X,Y)=\mathbb{K}(X,Y)+{R}(X,Y).

We define

(6.7)

\mathbb{K}(X,Y):=\begin{pmatrix}K(X,Y)&-\partial_{Y}K(X,Y)\\
-\partial_{X}K(X,Y)&\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}K(X,Y)\end{pmatrix}

with

(6.8)

{K}(X,Y):=\int\limits_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dZ}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dW}{2\pi i}\;e^{XZ+YW}\;G_{\alpha,\beta}(Z)\,G_{\alpha,\beta}(W)\,Q(Z,W)

where
d
satisfies
u<d<t
,
and define

(6.9)

\displaystyle{R}(X,Y)=\begin{pmatrix}A(X)B(Y)-A(Y)B(X)&-A(X)B^{\prime}(Y)+A^{\prime}(Y)B(X)\\
-A^{\prime}(X)B(Y)+A(Y)B^{\prime}(X)&A^{\prime}(X)B^{\prime}(Y)-A^{\prime}(Y)B^{\prime}(X)\end{pmatrix}

where
{R}
captures poles at
Z=-u
and
W=-u.

Proof.

We show the
11-
entry as an example. The overall idea is that since
u
will be sent to
-t<0
, the poles
z=-u
and
w=-u
have to cross the contour. Therefore, we isolate these two poles via direct evaluation of the residue and store them in the matrix
{R}
.
We see that

(6.10)

\displaystyle\text{Res}(Z=-u,\text{ integrand of }\eqref{eq: central kernel K})=A(X)B(Y)

\displaystyle\text{Res}(W=-u,\text{ integrand of }\eqref{eq: central kernel K})=-A(Y)B(X).

The remaining kernel is written as
K(X,Y)
which contains poles
\{-t-i,-\alpha-i,-\beta-i,-u-1-i:i\in\Z_{\geq 0}\}
for both
Z
and
W
, which is the reason for choosing
t>d_{0}>u
.
∎

Recall that
J(x,y)=\mathbbm{1}_{x=y}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}.

We have that

(6.11)

\displaystyle J^{-1}{R}=\left|\mathcal{X}_{1}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{1}\right|+\left|\mathcal{X}_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{2}\right|,

where

(6.12)

\mathcal{X}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}\\
A\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathcal{Y}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}B&-B^{\prime}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathcal{X}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}B^{\prime}\\
B\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathcal{Y}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-A&A^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}.

We define
\overline{G}=J^{-1}\mathbb{K}
and perform the following reformulation of Fredholm Pfaffian

(6.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pf}(J-{\mathbb{\check{K}}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\det(\mathbbm{1}-J^{-1}{\mathbb{\check{K}}})

\displaystyle=\det\left(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G}-\left|\mathcal{X}_{1}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{1}\right|-\left|\mathcal{X}_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{2}\right|\right)

\displaystyle=\det(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})\det\left(\mathbbm{1}-(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{1}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{1}\right|-(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{2}\right|\right)

\displaystyle=\det(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})\det\left(\mathbbm{1}-\begin{pmatrix}(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{1}\right\rangle&(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{2}\right\rangle\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{Y}_{1}\\
\mathcal{Y}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})\det\left(\mathbbm{1}-\begin{pmatrix}\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{1}\right|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{1}\right\rangle&\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{1}\right|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{2}\right\rangle\\
\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{2}\right|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{1}\right\rangle&\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{2}\right|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{2}\right\rangle\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})(1-\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{1}\right|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{1}\right\rangle)^{2}.

Since
\mathbb{K}
is antisymmetric, we see that

(6.14)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{1}\right|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{1}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{2}\right|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{2}\right\rangle,\quad\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{1}\right|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{2}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{2}\right|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{1}\right\rangle=0,

which justifies the last equality in (
6.13
). The fifth equality follows from
\det(\mathbbm{1}-AB)=\det(\mathbbm{1}-BA).

To simplify our notation, we define

\phi_{1}=(\overline{G}\mathcal{X}_{1})_{1},\quad\phi_{2}=(\overline{G}\mathcal{X}_{1})_{2}.

Lemma 6.3
.

Fix any
t\in(\frac{1}{N},\frac{1}{2}),

\alpha,\beta>t,
and
t>u>\frac{1}{N}.

The Fredholm Pfaffian can be reformulated into

(6.15)

\displaystyle\Pf(J-{\mathbb{\check{K}}})=\Pf(J-\mathbb{K})\left(1-\left\langle B\left|A^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle B^{\prime}\left|A\right\rangle\right.\right)-\Pf(J-\mathbb{K})

\displaystyle+\mathrm{Pf}\left(J-\mathbb{K}-\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\phi_{2}\\
-\phi_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle B\quad-B^{\prime}\right|-\left|\begin{array}[]{c}B\\
-B^{\prime}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\phi_{2}\quad\phi_{1}\right|\right).

Proof.

By
[
17
, Lemma
5.13
]
and (
7.9
), we know that
||\mathbb{K}||<1
when
N
is sufficiently large, which implies that
(\mathbbm{1}-J^{-1}\mathbb{K})^{-1}
can be viewed as a series expansion. We choose
N_{0}\in\Z_{\geq 3}
large enough such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
,
||\mathbb{K}||<1
.
By further decomposing the formula in (
6.13
), we get that

(6.16)

\displaystyle\Pf(J-{\mathbb{\check{K}}})=\Pf(J-\mathbb{K})\left(1-\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{1}\left|\mathcal{X}_{1}\right\rangle\right.-\left\langle\mathcal{Y}_{1}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\right|\overline{G}\mathcal{X}_{1}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\Pf(J-\mathbb{K})\left(1-\left\langle B\left|A^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle B^{\prime}\left|A\right\rangle\right.-\left\langle(B,\,-B^{\prime})\left|(\mathbbm{1}-J^{-1}\mathbb{K})^{-1}\right|\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{1}\\
\phi_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle\right):=\mu(N)

for
N\in\Z_{\geq N_{0}}
. Now, we view
\mu(N)
as a function of
N\in\mathbb{R}_{>N_{0}}
. We want to show that
\mu(N)
is a real-analytic function for
N\in(N_{0},\infty)
.
It suffices to justify that we may differentiate with respect to
N
under the contour integrals. We use
{K}
as an example. Let
H(Z,W)=\frac{\Gamma(u+Z)}{\Gamma(u-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(u+W)}{\Gamma(u-W)}\frac{\Gamma(t+Z)}{\Gamma(t-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(t+W)}{\Gamma(t-W)}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha-Z)}{\Gamma(\alpha+Z)}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha-W)}{\Gamma(\alpha+W)},
and
T_{0}(\alpha,Z,W)=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+Z)}{\Gamma(\alpha-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+W)}{\Gamma(\alpha-W)}.
Then

(6.17)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial N}{K}(X,Y)=\int\limits_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dZ}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dW}{2\pi i}\;e^{XZ+YW}H(Z,W)\frac{\partial}{\partial N}\left(T_{0}(\alpha,Z,W)\right)^{N}\,Q(Z,W),

where

(6.18)

\frac{\partial}{\partial N}\left(T_{0}(\alpha,Z,W)\right)^{N}=\log\left(T_{0}(\alpha,Z,W)\right)e^{N\log(T_{0}(\alpha,Z,W))}.

Consider the change of variables
W=-d+{\rm i}w,
and
Z=-d+{\rm i}v
. Then (
7.3
) implies that for any
m\in\Z_{>0}
,

(6.19)

\bigg|\frac{\partial^{m}}{\partial N^{m}}\left(T_{0}(\alpha,Z,W)\right)^{N}\bigg|\leq\frac{2d\left(\log((1+|v|)(1+|w|))\right)^{m}}{((1+|v|)(1+|w|))^{2dN}},

hence the integrand in (
6.17
) is bounded by an
N
-independent integrable function of
(v,w)
:

(6.20)

\displaystyle Ce^{-d(X+Y)-4d}e^{-|v+w|\pi}\frac{2d\left(\log((1+|v|)(1+|w|))\right)^{m}}{((1+|v|)(1+|w|))^{2d(N_{0}+1)}}(1+|2v|)^{2d-\frac{1}{2}}(1+|2w|)^{2d-\frac{1}{2}},

for some constant
C>0
. Since
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\log x\right)^{m}x^{-2}dx<\infty
for any fixed
m
, this bound is integrable in
(v,w)
. This bound uses the same argument as in (
7.7
). Therefore, by dominated convergence, we may pass
\partial/\partial N
inside the
Z,W
-integrals in (
6.17
). This proves that
{K}(X,Y)
(hence
\mu(N)
) depends real-analytically on
N\in\mathbb{R}_{>N_{0}}
.
By
[
19
, Lemma 6.10]
, we can rewrite the bracket term as the difference of two Fredholm Pfaffians as follows:

(6.21)

\displaystyle\mu(N)=\Pf(J-\mathbb{K})\left(1-\left\langle B\left|A^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle B^{\prime}\left|A\right\rangle\right.\right)-\Pf(J-\mathbb{K})\left\langle(B,\,-B^{\prime})\left|(\mathbbm{1}-J^{-1}\mathbb{K})^{-1}\right|\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{1}\\
\phi_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\text{RHS of }\eqref{eq: Pfreformulate}=:\nu(N).

Since
(
6.15
)
does not require the well-definedness of
(\mathbbm{1}-J^{-1}K)^{-1}
, we have
\nu(N)
as the natural analytic continuation of
\mu(N)
to
N\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 3}.
Combining (
6.16
) and (
6.21
) gives the equality of two formulas in (
6.15
) for all
N\in\Z_{\geq 3}
.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

Lemma
6.3
is designed to remove the resolvent
(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}
because it is difficult to show its limit under the critical scaling exists. We also have a slightly modified version of (
6.15
). By
[
19
, Remark 6.13]
, we know that for any constant
c_{0}

(6.22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pf}(J-\mathbb{K})

\displaystyle c_{0}\left\langle(B,\,-B^{\prime})\left|(\mathbbm{1}-J^{-1}\mathbb{K})^{-1}\right|\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{1}\\
\phi_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pf}(J-\mathbb{K})-\mathrm{Pf}\left(J-\mathbb{K}-c_{0}\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\phi_{2}\\
-\phi_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle B\quad-B^{\prime}\right|-c_{0}\left|\begin{array}[]{c}B\\
-B^{\prime}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\phi_{2}\quad\phi_{1}\right|\right).

7.
Analytic continuation of two parameter stationary formula when
\beta>t

We first analyze the
u\rightarrow-t
limit of the central kernel
\mathbf{K}
and its Fredholm Pfaffian.

Lemma 7.1
.

Fix any
\varepsilon\ll 1.

For any
\alpha>t,

t\in(\frac{1}{N},\frac{1}{2}),
and
\frac{1}{2}>\beta>t
,
X,Y\in\mathbb{R},
the kernel
\mathbb{K}(X,Y)
is analytic for
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
with the following limit:

(7.1)

\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\mathbb{K}(X,Y)=\widehat{\mathbb{K}}(X,Y).

Proof.

It suffices to discuss the case of
K(X,Y)
as the convergence of other entries follow analogously. Because of the kernel decomposition, the poles
Z=-u
and
W=-u
that were present in the kernel
{\mathbb{\check{K}}}
no longer exist in the kernel
\mathbb{K}
. The remaining
u
-dependent poles are
Z,W=-u-i
for
i\geq 1
, which stay to the left of the contour
-d+{\rm i}\mathbb{R}
for
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
.

The integrand is analytic in
u
as
\Gamma
functions are analytic on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,-1,\cdots\}
. To apply dominated convergence theorem, we need to bound the integrand of
K(X,Y)
uniformly for all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
. Recall the Stirling approximation for
\Gamma
function

(7.2)

|\Gamma(a+iu)|=\sqrt{2\pi}|u|^{a-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-a-\frac{|u|\pi}{2}}(1+\mathcal{O}(|u|^{-1})).

Let
W=-d+{\rm i}w
and
Z=-d+{\rm i}v
. Then there exists some constant
C>0
such that for all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
,

(7.3)

\left|\frac{\Gamma(u+Z)}{\Gamma(u-Z)}\right|=\left|\frac{\Gamma(u-d+{\rm i}v)}{\Gamma(u+d-{\rm i}v)}\right|\leq C(1+|v|)^{-2d}.

Moreover, since
\left|\sin(\pi z)\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}|e^{-{\rm i}\pi z}|(|e^{2{\rm i}\pi z}|+1)
, we have that

(7.4)

\displaystyle\bigl|\sin\bigl(\pi{\rm i}(v-w)\bigr)\bigr|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\,e^{\pi(v-w)}\left(e^{-2\pi(v-w)}+1\right)\leq C_{1}\left(e^{-\pi(v-w)}+e^{\pi(v-w)}\right).

for some
C_{1}>0
. Additionally, for some
C_{2}>0,

(7.5)

\left|\frac{1}{\sin(\pi(-2d+{\rm i}(v+w)))}\right|\leq C_{2}e^{-\pi|v+w|}.

Thus, we know that

(7.6)

\left|\frac{\sin(\pi(Z-W))}{\sin(\pi(Z+W))}\right|\leq C_{3}e^{-\pi|v+w|+\pi|v-w|}

for some
C_{3}>0
.
Therefore, there exists some constant
C_{4}>0
such that the integrand is upper bounded by

(7.7)

\displaystyle C_{4}e^{-d(X+Y)}(1+|v|)^{-2d(N+1)}(1+|w|)^{-2d(N+1)}(1+|2v|)^{2d-\frac{1}{2}}(1+|2w|)^{2d-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-4d-(|v+w|)\pi}

for all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
. In the cases of
\partial_{X}K(X,Y),\partial_{Y}K(X,Y),\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}K(X,Y)
, the upper bound will be multiplied by additional
|v|,|w|,|vw|
. But since
d>\frac{1}{N}
, all the upper bounds are integrable in
u
and
v
.
∎

Lemma 7.2
.

Fix any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and any
\varepsilon\ll 1.
For any
t\in(\frac{1}{N},\frac{1}{2}),

\alpha>t,
and
\beta>t
, we have that
\Pf\left(J-\mathbb{K}\right)
is analytic for
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
with the following well-defined limit:

(7.8)

\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Pf(J-\mathbb{K})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\tau,\infty)}=\Pf(J-\widehat{\mathbb{K}})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\tau,\infty)}.

Proof.

From (
7.7
), we see that for all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon),

(7.9)

|K(X,Y)|\leq\frac{C}{2Nd}e^{-d(X+Y)}

where
C>0
is a constant independent of
X,Y
. Using the same argument, we get that for all
X,Y\geq\tau,

(7.10)

|K(X,Y)|,|\partial_{X}K(X,Y)|,|\partial_{Y}K(X,Y)|,|\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}K(X,Y)|\leq\frac{C}{2Nd}e^{-d(X+Y)}.

By Hadamard’s bound and dominated convergence theorem, we conclude the limit.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Fix any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and any
\varepsilon\ll 1.
For any
t\in(\frac{1}{N},\frac{1}{2}),

\alpha>t,
and
\beta>t
, the following expression is analytic for
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
with a well-defined limit:

(7.11)

\begin{split}&\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Gamma(u+t)\left(1-\left\langle B\left|A^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle B^{\prime}\left|A\right\rangle\right.\right)=\widehat{C}.\\
\end{split}

Proof.

We have

(7.12)

\begin{split}&-\left\langle B\left|A^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle B^{\prime}\left|A\right\rangle\right.\\
&=\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}e^{-\tau(u-W)}\frac{u+W}{u-W}\frac{\Gamma(t-u)}{\Gamma(t+u)}G_{\alpha,\beta}(-u)G_{\alpha,\beta}(W)\frac{\Gamma(t+W)}{\Gamma(t-W)}\Gamma(-2W)\frac{\Gamma(1-u+W)}{\Gamma(1-u-W)}.\end{split}

Notice that a new pole
W=u
is introduced due to the inner product. The expression is not analytic when we take
u\rightarrow-t
as the pole will cross the contour. Thus, we move the contour to enclose
u
and then subtract the residue of the extra pole at
W=u
so that the expression is analytic in
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
. By simple computation, we find that
\text{Res}(W=u)=1
. Let
W=-d+{\rm i}w
. The integrand is upper bounded by the following

(7.13)

Ce^{-d\tau}(1+|w|)^{-2d(N+1)}(1+|2w|)^{2d-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-2d-|w|\pi}

for all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
and for some constant
C>0
. By dominated convergence theorem, we get (
7.11
).
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Fix any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and any
\varepsilon\ll 1.
For any
t\in(\frac{1}{N},\frac{1}{2}),

\alpha>t
, and
\beta>t
, the following expression is analytic for
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
with a well-defined limit:

(7.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Gamma(u+t)\phi_{1}(X)=\widehat{\phi}_{1}(X)

\displaystyle,\quad\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Gamma(u+t)\phi_{2}(X)=\widehat{\phi}_{2}(X),

\displaystyle\quad\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}B(X)=\widehat{B}(X)

\displaystyle,\quad\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}B^{\prime}(X)=\widehat{B}^{\prime}(X).

Proof.

Let us first prove
\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Gamma(u+t)\phi_{1}(X)=\widehat{\phi}_{1}(X)
and the rest follows analogously. We have

(7.15)

\displaystyle\Gamma(u+t){\phi}_{1}(X)

\displaystyle=\Gamma(t-u)G_{\alpha,\beta}(-u)\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dZ}{2\pi{\rm i}}\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}Z(t-W)e^{XZ}e^{-\tau(u-W)}\frac{\Gamma(u+Z)}{\Gamma(u-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(t+Z)}{\Gamma(t-Z)}

\displaystyle G_{\alpha,\beta}(Z)\frac{\Gamma(u+W)}{\Gamma(u-W+1)}\frac{\Gamma(t+W)}{\Gamma(t-W)}G_{\alpha,\beta}(W)\Gamma(-2Z)\Gamma(-2W)\frac{\sin(\pi(Z-W))}{\sin(\pi(Z+W))}.

Unlike Lemma
7.3
where a new pole
W=u
is created by taking inner product with
e^{-uY}
, the pole
W=u
in this case gets absorbed into the term
\Gamma(u-W)
and thus no new pole is introduced. Let
W=-d+{\rm i}w
,
Z=-d+{\rm i}v
and do the same analysis of upper bounds as in (
7.13
). The integrand is bounded by

(7.16)

\displaystyle Ce^{-dX}|vw|(1+|v|)^{-2d(N+1)}(1+|w|)^{-2d(N+1)-1}(1+|2v|)^{2d-\frac{1}{2}}(1+|2w|)^{2d-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-4d-(|v+w|)\pi}

for all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
, which allows us to apply dominated convergence theorem to get desired limiting functions.
∎

Recall remark
6.4
for the reason of placing
\Gamma(u+t)
inside the Pfaffian in the next lemma.

Lemma 7.5
.

Fix any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and any
\varepsilon\ll 1.
For any
t\in(\frac{1}{N},\frac{1}{2}),

\alpha>t,
and
\beta>t
, the following expression is analytic for
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
with a well-defined limit:

(7.17)

\begin{split}&\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}-\mathrm{Pf}\left(J-\mathbb{K}-\Gamma(u+t)\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\phi_{2}\\
-\phi_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle B\quad-B^{\prime}\right|-\Gamma(u+t)\left|\begin{array}[]{c}B\\
-B^{\prime}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\phi_{2}\quad\phi_{1}\right|\right)\\
&=-\mathrm{Pf}\left(J-\widehat{\mathbb{K}}-\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\phi}_{2}\\
-\widehat{\phi}_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\widehat{B}\quad-\widehat{B}^{\prime}\right|-\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{B}\\
-\widehat{B}^{\prime}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\widehat{\phi}_{2}\quad\widehat{\phi}_{1}\right|\right)\end{split}

where the Fredholm Pfaffian is taken over
\mathbb{L}^{2}((\tau,\infty))
.

Proof.

From the proof of Lemma
7.1
, we have that there exists
C>0
such that for all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
,

(7.18)

|K(X,Y)|,|\partial_{X}K(X,Y)|,|\partial_{Y}K(X,Y)|,|\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}K(X,Y)|\leq\frac{C}{2Nd}e^{-d(X+Y)}.

for some
C>0
independent of
X,Y
. To apply the Hadamard’s bound and dominated convergence theorem, we need the same type of upper bounds for

(7.19)

\phi_{2}(U)B(V),\phi_{2}(U)B^{\prime}(V),\phi_{1}(U)B(V),\phi_{1}(U)B^{\prime}(V)

where
(U,V)=(X,Y)
or
(Y,X)
. From the proof of Lemma
7.1
, we have the following upper bounds for all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
,

(7.20)

\phi_{1}(U),\phi_{2}(U)\leq\frac{C_{1}}{2Nd}e^{-dU},\quad B(V),B^{\prime}(V)\leq\frac{C_{2}}{2Nd}e^{-dV}

for some
C_{1},C_{2}>0
independent of
U,V
. Lastly, by Lemma
7.1
and Lemma
7.4
, we know that the Pfaffian kernels converges pointwise to their desired limits.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

We set
\beta=\alpha
and
\gamma=\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha-u)
. We will use the notation
Z^{u,-}_{\alpha>t}(N,N)
. Under this case, we have
Z^{u,-}_{\alpha>t}(N,N)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\Rightarrow}}Z^{t}(N,N)
when
u\rightarrow-t
. Reproving Lemmas
6.3
,
7.1
,
7.2
,
7.3
,
7.4
, and
7.5
with
\beta=\alpha
shows that RHS of (
1.8
) is the analytic continuation of the LHS of (
6.3
).
To prove the theorem, we simply need to show the following limit for the RHS of (
6.3
):

(7.21)

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\mathbbm{E}\bigg[2\left(e^{-\tau}Z^{u,-}_{\alpha>t}(N,N)\right)^{\frac{u+t}{2}}K_{-u-t}\left(2\sqrt{{Z^{u,-}_{\alpha>t}(N,N)e^{-\tau}}}\right)\bigg]=\mathbbm{E}\bigg[2K_{0}\left(2{e^{\left(\log Z^{t}(N,N)-\tau\right)/2}}\right)\bigg].

Let
\xi=e^{-\tau}.

Then using
e^{-y}\leq y^{-1}
, we get

(7.22)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\xi Z^{u,-}_{\alpha>t}(N,N)x}x^{-(u+t)-1}e^{-x^{-1}}dx\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{E}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}(\xi Z^{u,-}_{\alpha>t}(N,N)x)^{-1}x^{-(u+t)-1}e^{-x^{-1}}dx\right]=\mathbbm{E}\left[(\xi Z^{u,-}_{\alpha>t}(N,N))^{-1}\right]\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{-2-(u+t)}e^{-x^{-1}}dx.

for all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
. Let
Y_{1}\sim\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha-t)
,
Y_{2}\sim\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha+t)
,
Y_{i}\sim\text{Gamma}^{-1}(2\alpha)
for
i=3,\dots 2N-4
. For all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon),
we have

(7.23)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left[(\xi Z^{u,-}_{\alpha>t}(N,N))^{-1}\right]\leq\xi^{-1}\mathbbm{E}\left[Y_{1}^{-1}\right]\mathbbm{E}\left[Y_{2}^{-1}\right]\mathbbm{E}\left[Y_{3}^{-1}\right]^{4-2N}<\infty

because
Z^{u,-}_{\alpha>t}(N,N)\geq\prod_{i=1}^{2N-2}Y_{i}
, which is product of weights over a single up-right path. Hence,
we can apply the dominated convergence theorem to get (
7.21
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.10
.

We consider the case when
\gamma=1
and
Z^{u,-}_{\beta>t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\Rightarrow}}Z^{t,\beta}
when
u\rightarrow-t.
Again, by Lemmas
6.3
,
7.1
,
7.2
,
7.3
,
7.4
, and
7.5
, we found the analytic continuation of
\Gamma(u+t)\Pf(J-\check{\mathbb{K}})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\tau,\infty)}
and prove the
u\rightarrow-t
limit is RHS of (
1.13
).
Since the weight at
(2,1)
is
1
, we isolate the random variable
\omega_{2,1}
from the expectation and get

(7.24)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\bigg[2(e^{-\tau}Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)\omega_{2,1})^{\frac{u+t}{2}}K_{-u-t}\left(2{e^{\left(\log Z^{u,-}_{\beta>t}(N,N)\omega_{2,1}-\tau\right)/2}}\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta-t)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-e^{-\tau}Z^{u,-}_{\beta>t}(N,N)wx}e^{-x^{-1}}x^{-(u+t)-1}dx\bigg]w^{t-\beta-1}e^{-w^{-1}}dw.

We simply need to show that

(7.25)

\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\text{RHS of }\eqref{eq: two param beta large pre limit}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta-t)}\mathbbm{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{\infty}2K_{0}\left(2\sqrt{e^{-\tau}Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)w}\right)w^{t-\beta-1}e^{-w^{-1}}dw\bigg].

This can be justified by the same arguments as in (
7.22
) and (
7.23
). The only difference is that

(7.26)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\xi Z^{u,-}_{\beta>t}(N,N)wx}e^{-x^{-1}}x^{-(u+t)-1}dx\bigg]w^{t-\beta-1}e^{-w^{-1}}dw

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\bigg[{(-\xi Z^{u,-}_{\beta>t}(N,N))^{-1}}\bigg]\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}{e^{-x^{-1}}x^{-(u+t)-2}}dx\right)w^{t-\beta-2}e^{-w^{-1}}dw<\infty.

∎

8.
Asymptotic analysis for product stationary and two-parameter stationary formulas when
\tilde{\beta}>\tilde{t}

In this section, we perform steepest descent method for the asymptotic limit of (
1.13
) with
\beta
parameter involved. The case when
\beta=\alpha
with no scaling is easier and can be proved by nearly identical arguments. Therefore, we omit the detailed proof for (
1.10
).
We recall the notations for the digamma function and polygamma functions:

(8.1)

\psi(z)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(z)}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}\Gamma(z),\qquad\qquad\psi^{(n)}(z)=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\mathrm{d}z^{n}}\psi(z).

We define the following two constants:

(8.2)

f=2\psi(\alpha),\quad\sigma=-\psi^{(2)}(\alpha),

Given the mean
-Nf
and the fluctuation order
N^{-1/3},
we consider the following scaling of parameters near the critical value
0
:

(8.3)

\displaystyle\beta=\frac{\tilde{\beta}}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}},\quad t=\frac{\tilde{t}}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}},

\displaystyle Z=\frac{-\tilde{Z}}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}},\quad W=\frac{-\tilde{W}}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}},

\displaystyle X=-Nf+(\sigma N)^{1/3}u,\quad Y

\displaystyle=-Nf+(\sigma N)^{1/3}v,\quad\tau=-Nf+(\sigma N)^{1/3}s.

Definition 8.1
.

For any
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
, we define the following contours on the complex plane:

(8.4)

V(a;\theta;d)=\left(\{a+re^{{\rm i}\theta}:r>0\}\cup\{a-re^{-{\rm i}\theta}:r\leq 0\}\right)\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|\text{Re}(z)|\leq d\},

(8.5)

L(a;b)=\{a+k{\rm i}:|k|\geq b\}.

In the following lemmas, we start to use
(u,v)
as new variables for the asymptotics. We write

(8.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\widehat{K}(u,v)=(\sigma N)^{2/3}\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\widehat{K}(X,Y),

\displaystyle\widehat{K}(u,v)=\widehat{K}(X,Y)=\widehat{K}(-Nf+(\sigma N)^{1/3}u,-Nf+(\sigma N)^{1/3}v).

Lemma 8.2
.

Choose any
r>0.
Fix any
\alpha>0
,
\tilde{\beta}>\tilde{t},

\tilde{t}>0.
The following limit holds uniformly over
u,v\in[-r,r]
:

(8.7)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\widehat{K}(X,Y)=\widetilde{K}(u,v),\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}-(\sigma N)^{1/3}\partial_{X}\widehat{K}(X,Y)=-\partial_{u}\widetilde{K}(u,v)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}-(\sigma N)^{1/3}\partial_{Y}\widehat{K}(X,Y)=-\partial_{v}\widetilde{K}(u,v),\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\sigma N)^{2/3}\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\widehat{K}(X,Y)=\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\widetilde{K}(u,v)

Moreover, fix any
\tilde{\delta}\in(0,\tilde{t})
, there exists a constant
C
independent of
u,v
such that the following upper bounds hold for all
u,v\geq-r
:

(8.8)

\displaystyle|\widehat{K}(u,v)|,\,|\partial_{u}\widehat{K}(u,v)|,\,|\partial_{v}\widehat{K}(u,v)|,\,|\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\widehat{K}(u,v)|\leq Ce^{-\tilde{\delta}(u+v)}.

Proof.

After change of variables, we have that

(8.9)

\displaystyle\widehat{K}(u,v)=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dZ}{2\pi{\rm i}}\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}e^{N(h(Z)+h(W))+\sigma N^{1/3}(uZ+vW)}H(t,\alpha,Z,W)

where
h
is defined as the following function on the complex plane:

(8.10)

h(Z)=\log\Gamma(\alpha+Z)-\log\Gamma(\alpha-Z)-fZ,

and

(8.11)

\displaystyle H(t,\alpha,Z,W)=

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(-t+Z)}{\Gamma(-t-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(t+Z)}{\Gamma(t-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(\beta+Z)}{\Gamma(\beta-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(-t+W)}{\Gamma(-t-W)}\frac{\Gamma(t+W)}{\Gamma(t-W)}\frac{\Gamma(\beta+W)}{\Gamma(\beta-W)}

\displaystyle\Gamma(-2Z)\Gamma(-2W)\frac{\sin(\pi(Z-W))}{\sin(\pi(Z+W))}\left(\frac{\Gamma(\alpha-Z)}{\Gamma(\alpha+Z)}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha-W)}{\Gamma(\alpha+W)}\right)^{2}.

Notice that

(8.12)

h(0)=h^{\prime}(0)=h^{\prime\prime}(0)=0\quad\text{ and }\quad h^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)=2\psi^{(2)}(\alpha).

Recall that if
z\in\mathcal{C}
, then
\text{Re}\{\log(z)\}=\log|z|
. Thus

(8.13)

\text{Re}\{h(Z)\}=\log\left|\frac{\Gamma(\alpha+Z)}{\Gamma(\alpha-Z)}\right|-f\text{Re}\{Z\}.

Given the product representation of the Gamma function

(8.14)

\Gamma(z)=\frac{1}{z}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1+\frac{z}{n})^{-1}(1+\frac{1}{n})^{z},

we have that

(8.15)

\begin{split}\text{Re}\{h(-d+iy)\}&=\log\left|\frac{(\alpha+d-iy)}{(\alpha-d+iy)}\right|\left|\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(n+\alpha+d-iy)}{(n+\alpha-d+iy)}\right|\left|\left(\frac{n+1}{n}\right)^{-2d}\right|+fd.\end{split}

Now we take the derivative with respect to
y
and get that

(8.16)

\begin{split}\frac{d}{dy}\text{Re}\{h(-d+iy)\}&=\frac{d}{dy}\log\left|\frac{(\alpha+d-iy)}{(\alpha-d+iy)}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(n+\alpha+d-iy)}{(n+\alpha-d+iy)}\right|\\
&=\frac{d}{dy}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\log|n+\alpha+d-iy|-\log|n+\alpha-d+iy|\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{-4d(n+\alpha)y}{((n+\alpha-d)^{2}+y^{2})((n+\alpha+d)^{2}+y^{2})}.\end{split}

Because the above derivative is positive when
y<0
and negative when
y>0
, we deduce that
\text{Re}\{h(Z)\}
attains its maximum at
Z=-d
on the contour
-d+i\mathbb{R}
.
Additionally, for
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

(8.17)

\frac{d}{dx}h(x)=\psi(\alpha+x)+\psi(\alpha-x)-2\psi(\alpha).

Because
\psi(x)
is strictly concave for
x>0
, we know that
\frac{d}{dx}h(x)<0
. We see that for all
x\in(-d,0),

h(x)<0
and therefore, for all
z=x+{\rm i}\mathbb{R}
,
\text{Re}(h(z))<h(x)
. Then we can see that for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
small satisfying
b>a
, the integration contour
V(-a;2\pi/3;b)\cup L(-b;\sqrt{3}(b-a))
is a steepest descent path for
h(Z)
.

Thus, fix any
\delta\in(0,\tilde{t})
,
b>\tilde{t},
and set
a=\frac{\delta}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}
. By Cauchy’s theorem, we can
deform the contour
{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d
into
V(\frac{-\delta}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}};2\pi/3;b)\cup L(-b;\sqrt{3}(b-\frac{\delta}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}))
without crossing any poles. Given the descent path, we know that for
(Z,W)
on
L(-b;\sqrt{3}(b-\frac{\delta}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}))
, there exists some
\varepsilon>0
such that
h(Z)+h(W)<-\varepsilon<0
. Let
L=L(-b;\sqrt{3}(b-\frac{\delta}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}))
for shorter notation. Then we have

(8.18)

\begin{split}&\left|\int\limits_{L}\frac{dZ}{2\pi{\rm i}}\int\limits_{L}\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}e^{N\left(h(Z)+h(W)\right)+(\sigma N)^{1/3}(uZ+vW)}H(t,\alpha,Z,W)\right|\\
&\leq Ce^{-\varepsilon N}\int\limits_{L}\frac{dZ}{2\pi{\rm i}}\int\limits_{L}\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}e^{N\left(h(Z)+h(W)+\varepsilon\right)+(\sigma N)^{1/3}(uZ+vW)}H(t,\alpha,Z,W),\end{split}

where the double integrals are bounded due to the exponential decay in
\exp(N(h(Z)+h(W)+\varepsilon))
on
L\times L
. So only the integrals over
V(-\delta/{(\sigma N)^{1/3}};2\pi/3;b)
contribute to the limit.

We apply the change of variables

Z=-\frac{\tilde{Z}}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}},

W=-\frac{\tilde{W}}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}
. Since
e^{{\tilde{Z}}^{3}/3+{\tilde{W}}^{3}/3-u\tilde{Z}-v\tilde{W}}
decays exponentially
along the directions
\pm\pi/3
and
H(t,\alpha,Z,W)
exhibits at most polynomial
growth in
\tilde{Z},\tilde{W}
, the absolute value of the integrand is bounded by an integrable
function, allowing the contours to be extended to infinity. Hence, we see that

(8.19)

\displaystyle\widehat{K}(u,v)=\widetilde{K}(u,v)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\!\left((\sigma N)^{-1/3}\right)\right),\quad(\sigma N)^{1/3}\partial_{X}\widehat{K}(u,v)=\partial_{u}\widetilde{K}(u,v)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\!\left((\sigma N)^{-1/3}\right)\right),

\displaystyle(\sigma N)^{1/3}\partial_{Y}\widehat{K}(u,v)=\partial_{v}\widetilde{K}(u,v)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\!\left((\sigma N)^{-1/3}\right)\right),\,\,\,(\sigma N)^{2/3}\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\widehat{K}(u,v)=\partial_{u}\widetilde{K}(u,v)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\!\left((\sigma N)^{-1/3}\right)\right).

∎

Lemma 8.3
.

Choose any
\alpha>0,

\tilde{t}>0,

\tilde{\beta}>\tilde{t}
, and
s\in\mathbb{R}
and set
\tau=-Nf+(\sigma N)^{1/3}s.
We have the following limit of the Fredholm Pfaffian:

(8.20)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\Pf\left(J-\widehat{\mathbb{K}}\right)_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\tau,\infty)}=\Pf\left(J-\widetilde{\mathbb{K}}\right)_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,\infty)}.

Proof.

Let
\rho=(\sigma N)^{-1/3}.
We have

(8.21)

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\widehat{\mathbb{K}}\right)_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\tau,\infty)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\int_{s}^{\infty}\cdots\int_{s}^{\infty}\Pf\left(\begin{pmatrix}\widehat{K}&-\rho^{-1}\partial_{Y}\widehat{K}\\
-\rho^{-1}\partial_{X}\widehat{K}&\rho^{-2}\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\widehat{K}\end{pmatrix}(u_{i},u_{j})\right)_{i,j=1}^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{n}du_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\int_{s}^{\infty}\cdots\int_{s}^{\infty}(2n)^{n/2}C^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{-2\delta u_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}du_{i}<\infty.

By dominated convergence theorem and the limit of the kernel in Lemma
8.2
, we get the convergence of Fredholm Pfaffian.
∎

Lemma 8.4
.

Choose any
r>0.
For any fixed
\alpha,\beta>0,

\tilde{t}>0,
the following limit holds uniformly over
u\in[-r,r],

(8.22)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\widehat{C}=\widetilde{C},\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\widehat{B}(X)=\widetilde{B}(u),\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\sigma N)^{1/3}\widehat{B}^{\prime}(X)=\widetilde{B}^{\prime}(u),

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\widehat{\phi}_{1}(X)=\widetilde{\phi}_{1}(u),\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\widehat{\phi}_{2}(X)=\widetilde{\phi}_{2}(u).

Proof.

The convergences follow the same steepest descent method as in the proof of Lemma
8.2
.
∎

Lemma 8.5
.

Choose any
\alpha>0,

\tilde{t}>0
,
\tilde{\beta}>\tilde{t}
, and any
s\in\mathbb{R}
with
\tau=-Nf+(\sigma N)^{1/3}s.
We have the following limit:

(8.23)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Pf}\left(J-\widehat{\mathbb{K}}-(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\phi}_{2}\\
-\widehat{\phi}_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\widehat{B}\quad-\widehat{B}^{\prime}\right|-(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{B}\\
-\widehat{B}^{\prime}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\widehat{\phi}_{2}\quad\widehat{\phi}_{1}\right|\right)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{Pf}\left(J-\widetilde{\mathbb{K}}-\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widetilde{\phi}_{2}\\
-\widetilde{\phi}_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\widetilde{B}\quad-\widetilde{B}^{\prime}\right|-\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widetilde{B}\\
-\widetilde{B}^{\prime}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\widetilde{\phi}_{2}\quad\widetilde{\phi}_{1}\right|\right).

Proof.

By Lemma
8.2
, Lemma
8.4
and Lemma
16.1
, we get the pointwise limits and upper bounds of each function and the matrix kernel
\widehat{\mathbf{K}}
. To upgrade to the convergence of Fredholm Pfaffian, we use upper bounds
e^{-\delta u_{i}}
, Hadamard’s bound on kernels and the dominated convergence theorem as explained in Lemma
8.3
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

We first set
\beta=\alpha
and do not apply any scaling to
\alpha
. We then reprove Lemmas
8.2
,
8.3
,
8.4
, and
8.5
in this setting, which yields the corresponding limiting objects
\widetilde{C}_{L}
,
\widetilde{\phi}_{1}^{L}
,
\widetilde{\phi}_{2}^{L}
,
\widetilde{B}_{L}
,

\widetilde{\mathbf{K}}_{L}
.
∎

We will take the scaling limits of the following proposition
[
12
, Proposition E.1.]
to uniquely determine the distributions of
\frac{\log Z^{t}(N,N)+Nf}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}
and
\frac{\log Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)+Nf}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}
.

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
R
be a random variable and
\sigma
be a strictly positive constant such that there exists some
\delta>0
and
\mathbbm{E}[e^{-{\delta R/\sigma}}]<\infty
. Let

(8.24)

Q(x,\sigma)=\mathbbm{E}[2\sigma K_{0}(2e^{(R-x)/(2\sigma)})].

Then, we can express the cumulative distribution function of
R
as

(8.25)

\PP(R\leq r)=\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}\pi{\rm i}}\int\limits_{-\delta+i\mathbb{R}}\frac{d\xi}{\Gamma(-\xi)\Gamma(-\xi+1)}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}dxe^{x\xi/\sigma}Q(x+r,\sigma).

Proof of Theorem
1.8
.

For simpler notation, let
\mathcal{X}_{N}=\frac{\log Z^{t}(N,N)+Nf}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}.
We define
F_{N}(r):=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{X}_{N}\leq r\right).

By Theorem
13.5
, we know that for any
\epsilon>0,
there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
,
C>0
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
and
x>0
,

(8.26)

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{X}_{N}\leq x\right)\leq Ce^{-(1-\epsilon)2\tilde{t}x}

By
[
12
, (E.2)]
, we get that for any fixed
N
and for all
\xi\in\mathbb{C}
satisfying
\text{Re}(w)<0,

(8.27)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\,e^{vw}Q_{N,t}^{Low}(v+\tau)dv=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}F_{N}(u+\tau)e^{uw}\Gamma(-w+1)\Gamma(-w)du,

where
F(u)=\mathbb{P}\left(\log(Z^{t}(N,N))\leq u\right)
. Then applying the scaling

(8.28)

\tau=r(\sigma N)^{1/3}-Nf,\quad v=x\sigma N^{1/3},\quad w=\xi/\sigma N^{1/3},\quad u=z\sigma N^{1/3}.

with
\xi=-A+{\rm i}y
gives

(8.29)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-Ax}e^{{\rm i}xy}\frac{{Q}_{N,t}^{Low}((x+r)\sigma N^{1/3}-Nf)}{\Gamma(-\xi/(\sigma N^{1/3}))\Gamma(-\xi/(\sigma N^{1/3})+1)}dx

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}F_{N}(z+r)e^{-Az}e^{{\rm i}zy}dz.

Since the sequence of random variables
\left(\mathcal{X}_{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight by Theorem
13.5
and Theorem
14.5
, we know that by Prohorov’s theorem,
there exists a subsequence
{N_{k}}
and a limiting random variable
\mathcal{X}
with CDF
\widetilde{F}
such that

(8.30)

\widetilde{F}(z)=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}F_{N_{k}}(z).

for all continuity points of
\widetilde{F}
.
The limiting distribution also admits the same lower tail behavior, i.e., there exists
C>0,
such that for all
x>0,

(8.31)

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{X}\leq-x\right)\leq Ce^{-2\tilde{t}x}.

We recall that
\delta
is chosen with
0<\delta<\tilde{t}
for the steepest-descent contours in Lemma
8.2
.
By the lower tail and upper tail of
Q_{N,t}^{Low}
provided in Corollary
15.2
and Lemma
16.4
, we see that for any
\epsilon>0,
there exists
c,C>0
,
N_{0}
and
s_{0}>0
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
,

(8.32)

\displaystyle(\sigma N)^{-1/3}Q_{N,t}^{Low}((x+r)(\sigma N)^{1/3}-Nf)\leq C|x|e^{-(1-\epsilon)2\tilde{t}|x|}\quad\text{for all }x<0,

\displaystyle(\sigma N)^{-1/3}Q_{N,t}^{Low}((x+r)(\sigma N)^{1/3}-Nf)\leq Ce^{(\tilde{t}-\delta)|x|}\quad\text{ for all }x>x_{0}.

Then fix some
\epsilon\ll 1
such that
(1-\epsilon)2\tilde{t}>\tilde{t}-\delta
. We need to pick
A
such that
(1-\epsilon)2\tilde{t}>A>\tilde{t}-\delta
because
e^{-Ax}
needs to deay faster than the right tail
e^{(\tilde{t}-\delta)}|x|
for
x>x_{0}
and grows slower than the left tail
|x|e^{-(1-\epsilon)2\tilde{t}|x|}
for
x<0
.
By the dominated convergence theorem, we can take the subsequential limit of (
8.29
) and obtain

(8.33)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}(A-{\rm i}y)e^{-Ax}e^{{\rm i}xy}\widetilde{{Q}}_{\tilde{t}}^{Low}(x+r)dx=\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{F}(z+r)e^{-Az}e^{{\rm i}zy}dz.

Since
\widetilde{F}(z+r)e^{-Az}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we can apply the Fourier inversion to obtain

(8.34)

\widetilde{F}(r)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(A-{\rm i}y)e^{-A{x}}e^{{\rm i}{x}y}\widetilde{{Q}}_{\tilde{t}}^{Low}(x+r)dxdy\quad\text{a.s.}

Since the limiting function is a CDF, it is uniquely characterized by the RHS of (
8.34
). Then by Prohorov’s theorem, we see that the full sequence
\left(\frac{\log Z(N,N)+Nf}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}\right)_{N\geq N_{0}}
converges weakly to
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.11
.

This theorem is a direct consequence of Lemmas
8.2
,
8.3
,
8.4
,
8.5
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.12
.

Fix any
\tilde{\beta}
,
\tilde{t}
with
\tilde{\beta}>\tilde{t}.

By the tightness proved in Theorem
13.6
and Theorem
14.5
, we know that there exists a limiting random variable
\mathcal{X}_{\tilde{\beta}}
such that
\frac{\log Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)+Nf}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}
converges weakly along a subsequence. Define
\widetilde{F}_{\tilde{\beta}}(r)=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\tilde{\beta}}\leq r).

We can use the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
1.8
since
{Q}_{N,\beta>t}^{High}
and
{Q}_{N,t}^{Low}
have the same lower tail and upper tail by Corollary
15.2
and Lemma
16.4
. Fix some
1\gg\epsilon>0
such that
(1-2\epsilon)2\tilde{t}>\tilde{t}-\delta
. Notice that in the steepest–descent analysis, the parameter
\delta
can be chosen arbitrarily close to
\tilde{t}
. Then we need to choose
A
such that
(1-\epsilon)2\tilde{t}>A>\tilde{t}-\delta>0
. By dominated convergence theorem, we obtain the limit

(8.35)

\displaystyle\frac{(A-{\rm i}y)}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{Q}_{\tilde{\beta}>\tilde{t}}^{High}(x+r-u)e^{-Ax}e^{{\rm i}xy}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\widetilde{F}_{\tilde{\beta}}(x+r-u)(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})e^{-(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})u}e^{-Ax}e^{{\rm i}xy}dudx,

Under the scaling,
(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\text{Gamma}^{-1}((\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})/(\sigma N)^{1/3})
converges to
\text{Exp}(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})
.
Applying the fourier inversion and the shift argument for removing an independent exponential random variable, which can be found in
[
11
, Lemma 3.3]
, to the RHS of (
12.11
) yields

(8.36)

\displaystyle\left(1+\frac{1}{\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t}}\partial_{r}\right)\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\widetilde{F}_{\tilde{\beta}}(x+r-u)(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})e^{-(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})u}e^{-Ax}e^{{\rm i}xy}dudxdy

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{1}{\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t}}\partial_{r}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{X}_{\tilde{\beta}}+\text{Exp}(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})\leq r\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{X}_{\tilde{\beta}}\leq r\right).

Since the LHS of (
8.36
) uniquely characterizes the limiting distribution, the subsequential weak convergence can be upgraded to full sequence convergence.
By (
12.11
) and (
8.36
), we get the following identity under the condition
\tilde{\beta}>\tilde{t}
:

(8.37)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{X}_{\tilde{\beta}}\leq r\right)=\left(1+\frac{1}{\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t}}\partial_{r}\right)\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(A-{\rm i}y)\widetilde{Q}_{\tilde{\beta}>\tilde{t}}^{High}(x+r)e^{-Ax}e^{{\rm i}xy}dxdy.

∎

9.
Two-parameter stationary case when
\beta<t

Recall
Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)
in Definition
4.4
and
Z^{u}_{\beta<t}(N,N)
in Definition
4.6
.

9.1.
Pfaffian decomposition for the two-parameter stationary model when
\beta<t

By Theorem
5.1
, we have the following Fredholm Pfaffian identity for
\frac{1}{N}<u,\beta<t
:

(9.1)

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-\tau}Z^{u}_{\beta<t}(N,N)}\right]=\Pf(J-\bm{\check{\mathcal{K}}})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}((\tau,\infty))},

where
\bm{\check{\mathcal{K}}}
is a
2\times 2
matrix kernel

(9.2)

\bm{\check{\mathcal{K}}}(X,Y)=\begin{pmatrix}\check{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)&-\partial_{Y}\check{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)\\
-\partial_{X}\check{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)&\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\check{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)\end{pmatrix}

and
\check{\mathcal{K}}
is the same function as defined in (
5.4
).
Using the same argument as before, our goal is to find the analytic continuation of (
9.1
).
We temporarily set
u\in(1/N,t)
for the following kernel decomposition. The contour
-d+{\rm i}\mathbb{R}
is the vertical line from
-d-{\rm i}\infty
to
-d+{\rm i}\infty
such that
\max\{u,\beta,0\}<d<t
. Note that
u,\beta
will be tuned to negative values. We define the following auxiliary functions.
Recall definitions of
H_{\alpha}(x),

F(x),

Q(x,y)
in (
3.21
).

(9.3)

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}(X)

\displaystyle:=F(-u)H_{\alpha}(-u)e^{-Xu},\quad\quad\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(X):=(-u)F(-u)H_{\alpha}(-u)e^{-Xu}.

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}(Y)

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}e^{YW}F(W)H_{\alpha}(W)\Gamma(-2W)\frac{\Gamma(1-u+W)}{\Gamma(1-u-W)},

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}(Y)

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}We^{YW}F(W)H_{\alpha}(W)\Gamma(-2W)\frac{\Gamma(1-u+W)}{\Gamma(1-u-W)}.

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}(X)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\Gamma(t-\beta)}{\Gamma(t+\beta)}\frac{\Gamma(u-\beta)}{\Gamma(u+\beta)}\left(\frac{\Gamma(\alpha-\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}\right)^{N-2}\!\!\!e^{-X\beta},\quad\mathsf{C}^{\prime}(X):=-\beta\frac{\Gamma(t-\beta)}{\Gamma(t+\beta)}\frac{\Gamma(u-\beta)}{\Gamma(u+\beta)}\left(\frac{\Gamma(\alpha-\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}\right)^{N-2}\!\!\!e^{-X\beta}.

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}(Y)

\displaystyle:=\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}e^{YW}\frac{\Gamma(t+W)}{\Gamma(t-W)}\frac{\Gamma(u+W)}{\Gamma(u-W)}H_{\alpha}(W)\Gamma(-2W)\frac{\Gamma(1-\beta+W)}{\Gamma(1-\beta-W)}+e^{-uY}H_{\alpha}(-u)\frac{\Gamma(t-u)}{\Gamma(t+u)}\frac{\Gamma(1-\beta-u)}{\Gamma(1-\beta+u)},

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}^{\prime}(Y)

\displaystyle:=\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}We^{YW}H_{\alpha}(W)\Gamma(-2W)\frac{\Gamma(t+W)}{\Gamma(t-W)}\frac{\Gamma(u+W)}{\Gamma(u-W)}H_{\alpha}(W)\frac{\Gamma(1-\beta+W)}{\Gamma(1-\beta-W)}

\displaystyle-ue^{-uY}\frac{\Gamma(t-u)}{\Gamma(t+u)}H_{\alpha}(-u)\frac{\Gamma(1-\beta-u)}{\Gamma(1-\beta+u)}.

Let

(9.4)

\bm{{\mathcal{K}}}(X,Y)=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)&-\partial_{Y}{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)\\
-\partial_{X}{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)&\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)\end{pmatrix},

where

(9.5)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dZ}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dW}{2\pi i}\;e^{XZ+YW}\;\frac{\Gamma(u+Z)}{\Gamma(u-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(u+W)}{\Gamma(u-W)}\frac{\Gamma(\beta+Z)}{\Gamma(\beta-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(\beta+W)}{\Gamma(\beta-W)}

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(t+Z)}{\Gamma(t-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(t+W)}{\Gamma(t-W)}H_{\alpha}(Z)\,H_{\alpha}(W)\,Q(Z,W).

We define

(9.6)

\displaystyle X_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\\
\mathsf{A}\end{array}\right),\quad Y_{1}=\left(\mathsf{B}\quad-\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\right),\quad X_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\\
\mathsf{B}\end{array}\right),\quad Y_{2}=\left(-\mathsf{A}\quad\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\right),

\displaystyle X_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\\
\mathsf{C}\end{array}\right),\quad Y_{3}=\left(\mathsf{D}\quad-\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\right),\quad X_{4}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\\
\mathsf{D}\end{array}\right),\quad Y_{4}=\left(-\mathsf{C}\quad\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\right).

We know that

(9.7)

\displaystyle J^{-1}\bm{\check{\mathcal{K}}}=J^{-1}{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}+\left|X_{1}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{1}\right|+\left|X_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{2}\right|+\left|X_{3}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{3}\right|+\left|X_{4}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{4}\right|.

At this stage of the decomposition, we subtract from
\bm{\mathcal{K}}
the contributions of the poles at
Z=-\beta,-u
and
W=-\beta,-u
. Corresponding residues have already been evaluated and stored in
X_{i},Y_{j}
. Now,
\bm{\mathcal{K}}
contain poles
\{-u-1-i,-\beta-1-i,-t-i,-\alpha-i:i\in\Z_{\geq 0}\}
. Then
\bm{\mathcal{K}}
can be analytically extended to
\beta\in(-t,t)
since
-\beta-1<t-1<-d
.
Then we decompose the kernel as follows. Let
\overline{G}=J^{-1}{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}
. We have

(9.8)

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\bm{\check{\mathcal{K}}}\right)^{2}=\det\left(\mathbbm{1}-J^{-1}\bm{\check{\mathcal{K}}}\right)=\det\left(\mathbbm{1}-J^{-1}{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}-\left|X_{1}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{1}\right|-\left|X_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{2}\right|-\left|X_{3}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{3}\right|-\left|X_{4}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{4}\right|\right)

\displaystyle=\det(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})\det\left(\mathbbm{1}-\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{1}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{1}\right|-\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{2}\right|-\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{3}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{3}\right|-\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{4}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{4}\right|\right)

\displaystyle=\det(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})\det\left(\mathbbm{1}-\begin{pmatrix}\left\langle Y_{1}\right|\\
\left\langle Y_{2}\right|\\
\left\langle Y_{3}\right|\\
\left\langle Y_{4}\right|\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{1}\right\rangle&\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{2}\right\rangle&\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{3}\right\rangle&\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{4}\right\rangle\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})

\displaystyle\det\left(\mathbbm{1}-\begin{pmatrix}\left\langle Y_{1}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{1}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{1}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{2}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{1}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{3}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{1}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{4}\right\rangle\right.\\
\left\langle Y_{2}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{1}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{2}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{2}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{2}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{3}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{2}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{4}\right\rangle\right.\\
\left\langle Y_{3}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{1}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{3}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{2}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{3}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{3}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{3}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{4}\right\rangle\right.\\
\left\langle Y_{4}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{1}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{4}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{2}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{4}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{3}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{4}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{4}\right\rangle\right.\\
\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})

\displaystyle\det\left(\mathbbm{1}-\begin{pmatrix}\left\langle Y_{1}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{1}\right\rangle\right.&0&\left\langle Y_{1}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{3}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{1}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{4}\right\rangle\right.\\
0&\left\langle Y_{1}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{1}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{2}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{3}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{2}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{4}\right\rangle\right.\\
\left\langle Y_{3}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{1}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{3}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{2}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{3}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{3}\right\rangle\right.&0\\
\left\langle Y_{4}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{1}\right\rangle\right.&\left\langle Y_{4}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{2}\right\rangle\right.&0&\left\langle Y_{4}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{4}\right\rangle\right.\\
\end{pmatrix}\right).

By the anti-symmetry of the kernel
\bm{{\mathcal{K}}}
, we have

(9.9)

\left\langle Y_{1}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{2}\right\rangle\right.=\left\langle Y_{2}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{1}\right\rangle\right.=\left\langle Y_{3}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{4}\right\rangle\right.=\left\langle Y_{4}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}X_{3}\right\rangle\right.=0,

as well as the following identities:

(9.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=1-\left\langle Y_{1}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\right|X_{1}\right\rangle=1-\left\langle Y_{2}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\right|X_{2}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\left\langle Y_{1}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\right|X_{3}\right\rangle=\left\langle Y_{4}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\right|X_{2}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}=\left\langle Y_{1}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\right|X_{4}\right\rangle=-\left\langle Y_{3}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\right|X_{2}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}=\left\langle Y_{2}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\right|X_{3}\right\rangle=-\left\langle Y_{4}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\right|X_{1}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}=\left\langle Y_{2}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\right|X_{4}\right\rangle=\left\langle Y_{3}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\right|X_{1}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\NN=1-\left\langle Y_{3}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\right|X_{3}\right\rangle=1-\left\langle Y_{4}\left|(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})^{-1}\right|X_{4}\right\rangle.

Then the previous equation becomes the following:

(9.11)

\displaystyle\det(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})\det\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{M}&0&\mathcal{A}&\mathcal{B}\\
0&\mathcal{M}&\mathcal{C}&\mathcal{D}\\
\mathcal{D}&-\mathcal{B}&\NN&0\\
-\mathcal{C}&\mathcal{A}&0&\NN\\
\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\det(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})\mathcal{M}^{2}\det\left(\begin{pmatrix}\NN&0\\
0&\NN\end{pmatrix}-\mathcal{M}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{D}&-\mathcal{B}\\
-\mathcal{C}&\mathcal{A}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{A}&\mathcal{B}\\
\mathcal{C}&\mathcal{D}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})\mathcal{M}^{2}\det\left(\begin{pmatrix}\NN&0\\
0&\NN\end{pmatrix}-\mathcal{M}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{D}\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}&0\\
0&-\mathcal{C}\mathcal{B}+\mathcal{A}\mathcal{D}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det(\mathbbm{1}-\overline{G})\left(\mathcal{M}\NN-\mathcal{A}\mathcal{D}+\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}\right)^{2}.

Therefore,

(9.12)

\displaystyle\Pf(J-\bm{\check{\mathcal{K}}})=\Pf(J-{\bm{\mathcal{K}}})\left(\mathcal{M}\NN-\mathcal{A}\mathcal{D}+\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}\right).

Our next goal is to find the
u\rightarrow-t
limit of the following identity:

(9.13)

\displaystyle\Gamma(u+t)\Pf(J-\bm{\check{\mathcal{K}}})

\displaystyle=\Pf(J-{\bm{\mathcal{K}}})\Gamma(u+t)\left(\mathcal{M}\NN-\mathcal{A}\mathcal{D}+\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}\right).

Definition 9.1
.

We define the scalar functions
\aleph_{i},\psi_{i},\Theta_{i},\zeta_{i},\eta_{i},\theta_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
in terms of the following identities. For
X,Y\in\mathbb{R}
,

(9.14)

\displaystyle\left\langle Y_{1}\right|\overline{G}(X)=\left\langle{\aleph}_{1}(X)\quad{\aleph}_{2}(X)\right|,\quad\left\langle Y_{2}\right|\overline{G}(X)=\left\langle{\psi}_{1}(X)\quad{\psi}_{2}(X)\right|,\quad\left\langle Y_{3}\right|\overline{G}(X)=\left\langle{\Theta}_{1}(X)\quad{\Theta}_{2}(X)\right|,

\displaystyle\overline{G}\left|X_{1}\right\rangle(Y)=\left|\begin{array}[]{c}{\zeta}_{1}(Y)\\
{\zeta}_{2}(Y)\end{array}\right\rangle,\quad\quad\overline{G}\left|X_{3}\right\rangle(Y)=\left|\begin{array}[]{c}{\eta}_{1}(Y)\\
{\eta}_{2}(Y)\end{array}\right\rangle,\quad\quad\overline{G}\left|X_{4}\right\rangle(Y)=\left|\begin{array}[]{c}{\theta}_{1}(Y)\\
{\theta}_{2}(Y)\end{array}\right\rangle.

The next lemma explains the existence of
\left(\Pf(J-\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{K}}})\right)^{-1}
in (
1.18
). Multiplying each of
\mathcal{M},

\NN,

\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{D}
with
\Pf(J-\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{K}}})
converts them into the difference of two Fredholm Pfaffians.

Lemma 9.2
.

Fix any
N\in\Z_{\geq 3}.
For any
t\in(\frac{1}{N},\frac{1}{2}),

\alpha>t
,
\beta\in(-t,t),
and
t>u>\frac{1}{N},

we have

(9.15)

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)\Gamma(u+t)\mathcal{M}=

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)\Gamma(u+t)\left(1-\left\langle Y_{1}\left|X_{1}\right\rangle\right.-\left\langle Y_{1}\left|\overline{G}\right|X_{1}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle-\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)+\Pf\left(J-{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}-\Gamma(u+t)\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta_{2}\\
-\zeta_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\aleph_{1}\quad\aleph_{2}\right|-\Gamma(u+t)\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\aleph_{1}\\
\aleph_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\zeta_{2}\quad\zeta_{1}\right|\right).

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)\NN=

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)\left(1-\left\langle Y_{3}\left|X_{3}\right\rangle\right.-\left\langle Y_{3}\left|\overline{G}\right|X_{3}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle-\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)+\Pf\left(J-{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}-\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\eta_{2}\\
-\eta_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\Theta_{1}\quad\Theta_{2}\right|-\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\Theta_{1}\\
\Theta_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\eta_{2}\quad\eta_{1}\right|\right).

(9.16)

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)\mathcal{A}=

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)\left(\left\langle Y_{1}\left|X_{3}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle Y_{1}\left|\overline{G}\right|X_{3}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle+\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)-\Pf\left(J-{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}-\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\eta_{2}\\
-\eta_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\aleph_{1}\quad\aleph_{2}\right|-\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\aleph_{1}\\
\aleph_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\eta_{2}\quad\eta_{1}\right|\right),

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)\mathcal{B}=

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)\left(\left\langle Y_{1}\left|X_{4}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle Y_{1}\left|\overline{G}\right|X_{4}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle+\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)-\Pf\left(J-{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}-\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\theta_{2}\\
-\theta_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\aleph_{1}\quad\aleph_{2}\right|-\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\aleph_{1}\\
\aleph_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\theta_{2}\quad\theta_{1}\right|\right),

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)\mathcal{C}=

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)\Gamma(u+t)\left(\left\langle Y_{2}\left|X_{3}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle Y_{2}\left|\overline{G}\right|X_{3}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle+\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)-\Pf\left(J-{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}-\Gamma(u+t)\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\eta_{2}\\
-\eta_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\psi_{1}\quad\psi_{2}\right|-\Gamma(u+t)\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\psi_{1}\\
\psi_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\eta_{2}\quad\eta_{1}\right|\right),

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)\mathcal{D}=

\displaystyle\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)\Gamma(u+t)\left(\left\langle Y_{2}\left|X_{4}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle Y_{2}\left|\overline{G}\right|X_{4}\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle+\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)-\Pf\left(J-{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}-\Gamma(u+t)\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\theta_{2}\\
-\theta_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\psi_{1}\quad\psi_{2}\right|-\Gamma(u+t)\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\psi_{1}\\
\psi_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\theta_{2}\quad\theta_{1}\right|\right).

Proof.

The RHS of the above equations are analytic continuations of the LHS for all
N\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 3}
. The proof follows the same arguments as in Lemma
6.3
. We see that
\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)(\mathcal{M}\NN-\mathcal{A}\mathcal{D}+\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C})
can be analytically extended to
\beta\in(-t,t)
because for all
\beta\in(-t,t)
, we have the following upper bounds for each function:

(9.17)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{K}(X,Y)|,\,|\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}(X,Y)|,\,|\partial_{X}\mathcal{K}(X,Y)|,\,|\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}(X,Y)|Ce^{-d(X+Y)}

\displaystyle|\aleph_{i}(X)|,\,|\psi_{i}(X)|,\,|\Theta_{i}(X)|,\,|\zeta_{i}(X)|,\,|\eta_{i}(X)|,\,|\theta_{i}(X)|\leq Ce^{-dX}

for all
X,Y\geq\tau
and for some
C>0
independent of
X,Y
. Therefore, all Fredholm Pfaffians and
\left\langle\cdot\left|\cdot\right\rangle\right.
(after evaluation) are analytic for
\beta\in(-t,t)
.
∎

To preview the analysis, section
11
will be devoted to prove the
u\rightarrow-t
limit of all formulas on the RHS of (
9.15
) and (
9.16
).

10.
Difference in kernel decompositions between log-gamma polymer and Geometric Last Passage Percolation

We now explain the intuition behind this kernel decomposition and why, in the log–gamma case, a substantially more complicated analysis is required. We begin with the geometric last passage model, where the decomposition is simpler. Let
\mathbf{1}
denote the matrix kernel with all entries
1
. For example, according to
[
19
, (6.37)]
, we have

(10.1)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}K_{11}^{geo}&K_{12}^{geo}\\
K_{21}^{geo}&K_{22}^{geo}\end{pmatrix}(k,\ell)\approx\begin{pmatrix}s^{k+\ell}&s^{k}r^{\ell}\\
s^{\ell}r^{k}&r^{k+\ell}\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{O}((\mathring{t})^{k+\ell})\mathbf{1},

where
s<1,

r\in(0,1/s),
and
\mathring{t}\rightarrow 1/s
to achieve the stationary geometric LPP.
For the log-gamma case,

(10.2)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\check{\mathcal{K}}&-\partial_{Y}\check{\mathcal{K}}\\
-\partial_{X}\check{\mathcal{K}}&\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\check{\mathcal{K}}\end{pmatrix}(X,Y)\approx\begin{pmatrix}e^{-t(X+Y)}&-te^{-t(X+Y)}\\
-te^{-t(X+Y)}&t^{2}e^{-t(X+Y)}\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-u(X+Y)})\mathbf{1}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\beta(X+Y)})\mathbf{1}.

The parameters are related by

(10.3)

\beta=-\log r,\quad t=-\log s,\quad u=-\log\mathring{t}

and the ranges correspond via

(10.4)

\displaystyle s<1

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad t>0,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{s}>r>s

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad-t<\beta<t.

In the geometric model we take the limit
\mathring{t}\rightarrow 1/s
, while in the log–gamma model we take
u\rightarrow-t.
In both cases, the terms
\mathcal{O}((\mathring{t})^{k+\ell})\mathbf{1}
and
\mathcal{O}(e^{-u(X+Y)})\mathbf{1}
become exploding terms that obstruct the convergence of the Fredholm Pfaffian. The key simplifying feature of the geometric kernel is its non-symmetric kernel structure. Specifically,
K_{11}^{geo}
decays exponentially, while
K_{22}^{geo}
grows exponentially. Since
r
cannot exceed
1/s
, the growth in
K_{22}^{geo}
is precisely compensated by the decay in
K_{11}^{geo}
. This imbalance allows the explosive term
r^{k},r^{\ell}
within the kernel to be controlled.

The situation changes fundamentally in the log–gamma case. The kernel is fully symmetric: permuting the parameters
t,\beta,u
does not change the kernel. Consequently, we can no longer use the decay in one entry to compensate for the growth in another. Instead, all four entries of the kernel either decay or grow simultaneously. Thus, the potential exponential growth from
\mathcal{O}(e^{-\beta(X+Y)})\mathbf{1}
and
\mathcal{O}(e^{-u(X+Y)})\mathbf{1}
must be isolated explicitly to guarantee convergence of the Fredholm Pfaffian. Concretely, in (
9.6
) ,
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
collect the
u-
poles and
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{C}^{\prime}
collect the
\beta-
poles. As a result, The rank-two representations

(10.5)

\mathcal{O}(e^{-u(X+Y)})\mathbf{1}=\left|X_{1}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{1}\right|+\left|X_{2}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{2}\right|,\quad\mathcal{O}(e^{-\beta(X+Y)})\mathbf{1}=\left|X_{3}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{3}\right|+\left|X_{4}\right\rangle\left\langle Y_{4}\right|

make these divergent contributions explicit and allow them to be factored out of the Pfaffian.

This analysis also clarifies why such a decomposition, (
9.6
) and (
9.7
), is unnecessary in the two-parameter stationary case when
\beta>t
as
\mathcal{O}(e^{-\beta(X+Y)})\mathbf{1}
decays exponentially and can remain in the kernel without affecting Pfaffian convergence.

11.
Analytic continuation of two parameter stationary formula when
\beta<t

11.1.
Analytic continuation of kernel and Fredholm Pfaffian

The following two lemmas prove limits of the central kernel, i.e.,
\bm{\mathcal{K}}
, and
\Pf\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}\right)
.

Lemma 11.1
.

Fix any
\varepsilon\ll 1.

For any
t\in(\frac{1}{N},\frac{1}{2}),

\alpha>t,

\beta\in(-t,0],

X,Y\in\mathbb{R},
the kernel
\bm{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)
is analytic for
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
with the following limit:

(11.1)

\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}(X,Y)=\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}(X,Y).

Proof.

The proof is completely analogous to the proof of Lemma
7.1
.
∎

Lemma 11.2
.

Fix any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and any
\varepsilon\ll 1.
For any
t\in(\frac{1}{N},\frac{1}{2}),

\alpha>t,

\beta\in(-t,t),
we have that
\Pf(J-{\bm{\mathcal{K}}})
is analytic for
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
with the following well-defined limit:

(11.2)

\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Pf(J-{\bm{\mathcal{K}}})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\tau,\infty)}=\Pf(J-{\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\tau,\infty)}.

Proof.

The proof is completely analogous to the proof of Lemma
7.2
. Specifically, there exists
C>0
independent of
X,Y,N
such that for all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
,

(11.3)

|\mathcal{K}(X,Y)|,|\partial_{X}\mathcal{K}(X,Y)|,|\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}(X,Y)|,|\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}(X,Y)|\leq\frac{C}{2Nd}e^{-d(X+Y)}

for all
X,Y\geq\tau.

Then we apply Hadamard’s bound.
∎

11.2.
Analytic continuation of
\mathcal{M}

For the following Lemma, recall definitions of
H_{\alpha},

F
, and
Q
in (
3.21
) and definitions of
\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{D}_{i},\mathcal{M}_{i},\NN_{i}
for
i=1,2
in (
3.22
), (
3.23
), (
3.24
), (
3.25
), (
3.26
), (
3.27
).

This lemma proves limits of all constant terms in Lemma
9.2
.

Lemma 11.3
.

Fix any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and any
\varepsilon\ll 1.
For any fixed
t\in(\frac{1}{N},\frac{1}{2}),

\alpha>t,

\beta\in(-t,t),
the following expressions are analytic for
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
with the well-defined limits:

(11.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Gamma(u+t)\left(1-\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\mathsf{A}\right\rangle\right.\right)=\mathcal{M}_{1}.

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Gamma(u+t)\left(-\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\partial_{X}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{A}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{A}\right\rangle\right)=\mathcal{M}_{2}.

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}1-\left\langle\mathsf{D}\left|\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\left|\mathsf{C}\right\rangle\right.=\NN_{1}.

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}-\left\langle\mathsf{D}\left|\partial_{X}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\left|\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathsf{D}\left|\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{C}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\left|\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{C}\right\rangle=\NN_{2}.

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.-\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\mathsf{C}\right\rangle\right.=\mathcal{A}_{1}.

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\partial_{X}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{C}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{C}\right\rangle=\mathcal{A}_{2}.

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.-\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\mathsf{D}\right\rangle\right.=\mathcal{B}_{1}.

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\partial_{X}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{D}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{D}\right\rangle=\mathcal{B}_{2}.

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Gamma(u+t)\left(-\left\langle\mathsf{A}\left|\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\left|\mathsf{C}\right\rangle\right.\right)=\mathcal{C}_{1}.

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Gamma(u+t)\left(-\left\langle\mathsf{A}\left|\partial_{X}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\left|\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathsf{A}\left|\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{C}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\left|\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{C}\right\rangle\right)=\mathcal{C}_{2}.

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Gamma(u+t)\left(-\left\langle\mathsf{A}\left|\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\left|\mathsf{D}\right\rangle\right.\right)=\mathcal{D}_{1}.

\displaystyle\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Gamma(u+t)\left(-\left\langle\mathsf{A}\left|\partial_{X}\overline{L}\right|\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\left|\overline{L}\right|\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathsf{A}\left|\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\overline{L}\right|\mathsf{D}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\left|\partial_{Y}\overline{L}\right|\mathsf{D}\right\rangle\right)=\mathcal{D}_{2}.

Proof.

We need to multiply
\Gamma(u+t)
to all terms that contain
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
.
We prove
\mathcal{M}
as an example. When
u>0
, direct evaluation gives

(11.5)

\begin{split}&-\left\langle Y_{1}\left|X_{1}\right\rangle\right.=-\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\mathsf{A}\right\rangle\right.\\
&=\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}\frac{e^{-\tau(u-W)}(u+W)}{(u-W)}\frac{\Gamma(t-u)}{\Gamma(t+u)}\frac{\Gamma(\beta-u)}{\Gamma(\beta+u)}H_{\alpha}(-u)H_{\alpha}(W)F(W)\Gamma(-2W)\frac{\Gamma(1-u+W)}{\Gamma(1-u-W)}.\end{split}

Similar to the product stationary case in Lemma
7.3
, a new pole
W=u
is introduced to both integrands due to the inner product. The expression is not analytic when we take
u\rightarrow-t
as the pole will cross the contour. Thus, we move the contour to enclose
u
and then subtract the residue of the extra pole at
W=u
so that the expression is analytic in
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
. By direct computation, we find that
\text{Res}(W=u)=1
. Let
W=-d+{\rm i}w
. Using the following upper bound on the integrand

(11.6)

Ce^{-d\tau}(1+|w|)^{-2d(N+1)}(1+|2w|)^{2d-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-2d-|w|\pi}

for all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
, we conclude that

(11.7)

\begin{split}&\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Gamma(u+t)\left(1-\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.+\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\mathsf{A}\right\rangle\right.\right)=\mathcal{M}_{1}.\\
\end{split}

We remark that
\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\mathsf{D}^{\prime}\right\rangle\right.-\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\mathsf{D}\right\rangle\right.
also involves the
W=u
pole that is intentionally added and needs to be removed.
It is clear that

(11.8)

-\left\langle Y_{1}\left|\overline{G}\right|X_{1}\right\rangle=-\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\partial_{X}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{A}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\left|\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{A}\right\rangle.

We show the limit of the first term in (
11.8
). For simpler notation, we set

(11.9)

\displaystyle B(X)=\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\frac{dV}{2\pi{\rm i}}e^{XV}\text{Int}_{B}(V),\quad-\partial_{X}\mathcal{K}(X,Y)=\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dZ}{2\pi{\rm i}}\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}(-Z)e^{XZ}e^{YW}\frac{\Gamma(u+W)}{\Gamma(u-W)}\text{Int}_{\mathcal{K}}(Z,W),

where
\text{Int}_{B}(V)
denote the remaining integrand of
B
and
\text{Int}_{\mathcal{K}}(Z,W)
denote the remaining integrand of
\mathcal{K}
.
Then we first assume
u>0
and compute the integrals:

(11.10)

\displaystyle-\left\langle\mathsf{B}\left|\partial_{X}\mathcal{K}\right|\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-uF(-u)H_{\alpha}(-u)\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dV}{2\pi{\rm i}}\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dZ}{2\pi{\rm i}}\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}\text{Int}_{B}(V)(-Z)\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{X(V+Z)}dX\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{Y(W-u)}dY\frac{\Gamma(u+W)}{\Gamma(u-W)}\text{Int}_{\mathcal{K}}(Z,W)

\displaystyle=-uF(-u)H_{\alpha}(-u)\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dV}{2\pi{\rm i}}\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dZ}{2\pi{\rm i}}\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}\text{Int}_{B}(V)(-Z)\frac{e^{\tau(V+Z)}}{-(V+Z)}\frac{e^{\tau(W-u)}}{(u-W)}\frac{\Gamma(u+W)}{\Gamma(u-W)}\text{Int}_{\mathcal{K}}(Z,W),

where integrals of
X
and
Y
are well-defined because
\text{Re}(V+Z)=-2d
and
\text{Re}(W-u)=-d-u
. Let
