V=-d+{\rm i}v,

Z=-d+{\rm i}z
, and
W=-d+{\rm i}w
.
After careful estimation of the integrand as in Lemma
7.1
(
7.7
), we get the following upper bound:

(11.11)

\displaystyle Ce^{-(3d+u)\tau-6d}\left((1+|z|)(1+|w|)(1+|v|)\right)^{-2d(N+1)}\left((1+|2z|)(1+|2w|)(1+|2v|)\right)^{2d-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\pi(|v|+|z+w|)}.

The term
1/(u-W)
in the last step of (
11.10
) does not introduce a
W=u
pole because it is canceled by the Gamma function
(u-W)\Gamma(u-W)=\Gamma(1+u-W)
. Using the upper bound (
11.11
) and dominated convergence theorem, we get the
u\rightarrow-t
limit of one term. The limit
\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}-\Gamma(u+t)\left\langle Y_{1}\left|\overline{G}\right|X_{1}\right\rangle=\mathcal{M}_{2}
can be proved by applying above arguments to the other three terms.
∎

The following lemma provides limits of all Pfaffian terms in Lemma
9.2
.

Lemma 11.4
.

Fix any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and any
\varepsilon\ll 1.
For any
\alpha>t,

t\in(\frac{1}{N},\frac{1}{2}),
and
\beta\in(-t,t)
, the following expression is analytic for
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
with a well-defined limit:

(11.12)

\begin{split}&\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\mathrm{Pf}\left(J-\bm{\mathcal{K}}-A\right)=\mathrm{Pf}\left(J-\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}-\widehat{A}\right).\end{split}

where
A
is one of the following matrix kernels:

(11.13)

\displaystyle\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma(u+t)\zeta_{2}\\
-\Gamma(u+t)\zeta_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\aleph_{1}\quad\aleph_{2}\right|+\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\aleph_{1}\\
\aleph_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\Gamma(u+t)\zeta_{2}\quad\Gamma(u+t)\zeta_{1}\right|,

\displaystyle\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\eta_{2}\\
-\eta_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\Theta_{1}\quad\Theta_{2}\right|+\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\Theta_{1}\\
\Theta_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\eta_{2}\quad\eta_{1}\right|,

\displaystyle\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\eta_{2}\\
-\eta_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\aleph_{1}\quad\aleph_{2}\right|+\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\aleph_{1}\\
\aleph_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\eta_{2}\quad\eta_{1}\right|,

\displaystyle\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\theta_{2}\\
-\theta_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\aleph_{1}\quad\aleph_{2}\right|+\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\aleph_{1}\\
\aleph_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\theta_{2}\quad\theta_{1}\right|,

\displaystyle\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\eta_{2}\\
-\eta_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\Gamma(u+t)\psi_{1}\quad\Gamma(u+t)\psi_{2}\right|+\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma(u+t)\psi_{1}\\
\Gamma(u+t)\psi_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\eta_{2}\quad\eta_{1}\right|,

\displaystyle\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\theta_{2}\\
-\theta_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\Gamma(u+t)\psi_{1}\quad\Gamma(u+t)\psi_{2}\right|+\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma(u+t)\psi_{1}\\
\Gamma(u+t)\psi_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\theta_{2}\quad\theta_{1}\right|,

and
\widehat{A}
represents its corresponding limit under
u\rightarrow-t
. In particular,
\widehat{\psi_{i}},\widehat{\zeta_{i}},\widehat{\aleph_{i}},\widehat{\theta_{i}},\widehat{\eta_{i}},\widehat{\Theta_{i}}
represents the limit of
\Gamma(u+t)\psi_{i},\Gamma(u+t)\zeta_{i},{\aleph_{i}},{\theta_{i}},{\eta_{i}},{\Theta_{i}}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof.

We prove the limit of
\zeta_{2}
and limits of all other functions follow similarly. By definition,

(11.14)

\displaystyle\overline{G}\left|X_{1}\right\rangle=\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta_{1}\\
\zeta_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{X}\mathcal{K}&-\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\\
\mathcal{K}&-\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\end{pmatrix}\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\\
\mathsf{A}\end{array}\right\rangle=\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{X}\mathcal{K}\mathsf{A}^{\prime}-\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\mathsf{A}\\
\mathcal{K}\mathsf{A}^{\prime}-\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\mathsf{A}\end{array}\right\rangle.

All four terms
\partial_{X}\mathcal{K}\mathsf{A}^{\prime},

-\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\mathsf{A}
,
\mathcal{K}\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
, and
-\partial_{Y}\mathcal{K}\mathsf{A}
can be evaluated in a similar way. We compute one term to give an example. We assume
u>0
temporarily to compute the inner product
\mathcal{K}\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
and then take
u\rightarrow-t
.

(11.15)

\displaystyle\Gamma(u+t)\mathcal{K}\mathsf{A}^{\prime}=\Gamma(u+t)\mathcal{K}\left|\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\Gamma(u+t)(-u)F(-u)H_{\alpha}(-u)\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dZ}{2\pi{\rm i}}\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}e^{XZ}\int_{\tau}^{\infty}e^{Y(W-u)}dY\frac{\Gamma(u+Z)}{\Gamma(u-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(u+W)}{\Gamma(u-W)}\text{Int}_{\mathcal{K}}(Z,W)

\displaystyle=\Gamma(u+t)(-u)F(-u)H_{\alpha}(-u)\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dZ}{2\pi{\rm i}}\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}e^{XZ}e^{-\tau(u-W)}\frac{\Gamma(u+Z)}{\Gamma(u-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(u+W)}{\Gamma(1+u-W)}\text{Int}_{\mathcal{K}}(Z,W)

\displaystyle\rightarrow t\Gamma(2t)\frac{\Gamma(\beta+t)}{\Gamma(\beta-t)}H_{\alpha}(t)\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dZ}{2\pi{\rm i}}\!\!\!\int\limits_{{\rm i}\mathbb{R}-d}\!\!\!\frac{dW}{2\pi{\rm i}}e^{XZ}e^{\tau(t+W)}\frac{\Gamma(-t+Z)}{\Gamma(-t-Z)}\frac{\Gamma(-t+W)}{\Gamma(1-t-W)}\text{Int}_{\mathcal{K}}(Z,W),

where
\text{Int}_{\mathcal{K}}(Z,W)=F(Z)F(W)H_{\alpha}(Z)H_{\alpha}(W)Q(Z,W)
and we used
(u-W)\Gamma(u-W)=\Gamma(1+u-W)
in the second equality. In the last step, the limit is justified by dominated convergence theorem and an upper bound similar to (
7.7
), (
11.6
), and (
11.11
). Combining all four terms, we see that
\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Gamma(u+t)\overline{G}\left|X_{1}\right\rangle=\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\zeta}_{1}\\
\widehat{\zeta}_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle.
It is clear that there exists
C>0
independent of
X
such that for all
u\in(-t-\epsilon,-t+\epsilon)
and
X\geq\tau,

(11.16)

|\Gamma(u+t)\zeta_{1}(X)|\leq Ce^{-dX},\quad|\Gamma(u+t)\zeta_{2}(X)|\leq Ce^{-dX}.

Without showing the details, we also claim that there exists
C>0
independent of
X
such that for all
u\in(-t-\epsilon,-t+\epsilon)
,
X\geq\tau,
and
i\in\{1,2\}
,

(11.17)

\displaystyle|\Gamma(u+t)\psi_{i}(X)|\leq Ce^{-dX},\,\,\,|\aleph_{i}(X)|\leq Ce^{-dX},\,\,\,|\theta_{2}(X)|\leq Ce^{-dX},\,\,\,|\eta_{i}(X)|\leq Ce^{-dX},\,\,\,|\Theta_{2}(X)|\leq Ce^{-dX}.

Consequently, the convergence of Fredholm Pfaffian follows from (
11.17
), Lemma
11.1
, Lemma
11.2
, dominated convergence theorem and Hadamard’s bound.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.13
.

Recall that
\beta\in(-t,t)
.
By Lemmas
9.2
,
11.1
,
11.2
,
11.3
, and
12.4
, we showed the following limit exists provided that
\Pf\left(J-\widehat{\bm{{\mathcal{K}}}}\right)\neq 0,

(11.18)

\lim_{u\rightarrow-t}\Gamma(u+t)\Pf\left(J-\bm{\check{\mathcal{K}}}\right)=\frac{1}{\Pf\left(J-\widehat{\bm{{\mathcal{K}}}}\right)}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{M}}\widehat{\NN}-\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}+\widehat{\mathcal{B}}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}\right).

Assume temporarily
\beta>t
. By isolating the random variable at
(1,1)
and
(2,1)
, we get

(11.19)

\displaystyle\Gamma(u+t)\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-e^{-\tau}Z^{u,-}_{\beta<t}(N,N)\omega_{1,1}}\right]=\mathbbm{E}\bigg[2(\tau Z_{\beta<t}^{u,-}(N,N))^{\frac{u+t}{2}}K_{-u-t}\left(2\sqrt{\tau Z_{\beta<t}^{u,-}(N,N)}\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta-t)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbbm{E}\bigg[2(\tau Z_{\beta<t}^{u,-}(N,N)w)^{\frac{u+t}{2}}K_{-u-t}\left(2\sqrt{\tau Z_{\beta<t}^{u,-}(N,N)}w\right)\bigg]w^{t-\beta-1}e^{-w^{-1}}dw.

We want to analytically extend (
11.19
) to
\beta\in(-t,t)
and take
u\rightarrow-t
.
We need to show the following convergence as
u\rightarrow-t
when
\beta\in(-t,t)
:

(11.20)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta-t)}\int_{0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\tau Z^{u,-}_{\beta<t}(N,N)wx}e^{-x^{-1}}x^{-(u+t)-1}dx\bigg]w^{t-\beta-1}e^{-w^{-1}}dw

\displaystyle\rightarrow\frac{1}{\Gamma(\beta-t)}\mathbbm{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{\infty}2K_{0}\left(2\sqrt{e^{-\tau}Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)w}\right)w^{t-\beta-1}e^{-w^{-1}}dw\bigg].

The difficulty in this case is that
w^{t-\beta-1}e^{-w^{-1}}
is no longer a probability density function and is itself no longer integrable when
\beta\in(-t,t)
. Let
\xi=e^{-\tau}.

By using
e^{-x}\leq x^{-1}
, we see that for all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon),

(11.21)

\displaystyle\text{LHS of }\eqref{eq: two param beta small prelimit}\leq\mathbbm{E}\bigg[\frac{1}{\xi Z^{u,-}_{\beta<t}(N,N)}\bigg]\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-x^{-1}}x^{-(u+t)-2}dx\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}w^{t-\beta-2}e^{-w^{-1}}dw<\infty

since
t<1/2
and
\beta\in(-t,t)
implies that
-2<t-\beta-2<2t-2<-1
.
Let
Y_{i}\sim\text{Gamma}^{-1}(2\alpha)
for
i=1,\dots,2N-3
. By Lemma
13.1
, we get for all
u\in(-t-\varepsilon,-t+\varepsilon)
,
Z^{u,-}_{\beta<t}\geq_{\mathrm{st}}\prod_{i=1}^{2N-3}Y_{i}
and

(11.22)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\bigg[\left(Z^{u,-}_{\beta<t}(N,N)\right)^{-1}\bigg]\leq\mathbbm{E}\bigg[\frac{1}{Y_{1}Y_{2}\cdots Y_{2N-2}}\bigg]\leq\mathbbm{E}\bigg[Y_{1}^{-1}\bigg]^{2N-3}.

Hence, (
11.20
) is proved by dominated convergence theorem.

By similar arguments as in Lemma
16.6
, there exists
\tau_{0}>0
such that for all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
,
\Pf(J-\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{K}}})
is close to
1
. We know that
\Pf(J-\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{K}}})
is analytic on
\mathbb{R}
and strictly positive on
(\tau_{0},\infty)
. Hence
\Pf(J-\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{K}}})
can only have isolated zeroes. By the proof above, we have the equality in (
1.18
) provided that
\Pf(J-\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{K}}})_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\tau,\infty)}\neq 0
. Notice that
{Q}_{N,\beta<t}^{High}(\tau)
is an increasing function (
K_{0}
is a decreasing function) in
\tau
for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and hence is bounded by some constant on
(-\infty,\tau_{0}]
. Thus, any singularity of the RHS of (
1.18
) is removable and we can extend the equality of (
1.18
) to
\mathbb{R}
.
∎

12.
Asymptotic analysis of two parameter stationary formula when
\tilde{\beta}<\tilde{t}

12.1.
Asymptotic analysis of kernel and Fredholm Pfaffian

In this section, we consider the critical scaling as in
8.3
.

Lemma 12.1
.

Fix any
r>0.
Fix any
\alpha>0
,
\tilde{t}>0,

\tilde{\beta}\in(-\tilde{t},\tilde{t})
. The following limits hold uniformly for
u,v\in(-r,r)
:

(12.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\widehat{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)=\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(u,v),\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}-(\sigma N)^{1/3}\partial_{X}\widehat{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)=-\partial_{u}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}-(\sigma N)^{1/3}\partial_{Y}\widehat{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)=-\partial_{v}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(u,v),\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\sigma N)^{2/3}\partial_{X}\partial_{Y}\widehat{\mathcal{K}}(X,Y)=\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(u,v)

Fix any
\delta\in(\max\{\tilde{\beta},0\},\tilde{t})
. There exists a constant
C
independent of
u,v
such that the following upper bounds hold for all
u,v\geq-r
:

(12.2)

\displaystyle|\widehat{\mathcal{K}}(u,v)|,\,|\partial_{u}\widehat{\mathcal{K}}(u,v)|,\,|\partial_{v}\widehat{\mathcal{K}}(u,v)|,\,|\partial_{u}\partial_{v}\widehat{\mathcal{K}}(u,v)|\leq Ce^{-\delta(u+v)}.

As a result, we have the convergence of the Fredholm Pfaffian

(12.3)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\Pf\left(J-\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}\right)_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(\tau,\infty)}=\Pf\left(J-\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}\right)_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(s,\infty)}.

under the scaling
\tau=-Nf+(\sigma N)^{1/3}s
.

Proof.

We apply the same steepest descent analysis as in Lemma
8.2
. The only difference is that the contour has to satisfy
\max\{\tilde{\beta},0\}<\delta<\tilde{t}
since the pole
W=-\beta
and
Z=-\beta
are not included in
\widehat{\mathcal{K}}.
The convergence of the Fredholm Pfaffian then follows from the bound (
12.2
) using arguments in Lemma
8.3
.
∎

12.2.
Asymptotic analysis of
{\mathcal{M}}
,
{\NN}
,
{\mathcal{A}}
,
{\mathcal{B}}
,
{\mathcal{C}}
,
{\mathcal{D}}
,
\widehat{\aleph}
,
\widehat{\psi}
,
\widehat{\Theta}
,
\widehat{\zeta}
,
\widehat{\eta}
,
\widehat{\theta}

Lemma 12.2
.

Fix any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and consider the scaling
\tau=-Nf+(\sigma N)^{1/3}s.
For any fixed
\alpha>0,

\tilde{t}>0,

\tilde{\beta}\in(-\tilde{t},\tilde{t})
, the following limit holds

(12.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\mathcal{M}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{1},\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\mathcal{M}_{2}=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{2},\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\NN_{1}=\widetilde{\NN}_{1},\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\NN_{2}=\widetilde{\NN}_{2},

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{A}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1},\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{A}_{2}=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2},\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{B}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{1},\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{B}_{2}=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{2},

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\mathcal{C}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\mathcal{C}_{2}=\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2},\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\mathcal{D}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{1},\quad\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\mathcal{D}_{2}=\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}.

Proof.

For
\mathcal{M}_{1}
, the term
\Gamma(2t)\Gamma(-2W)dW
contributes a prefactor of
(\sigma N)^{1/3}
. Consequently,
\mathcal{M}_{1}
must be scaled by
(\sigma N)^{-1/3}.
similar scaling arguments apply to
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2}
. We then apply the steepest descent method as in Lemma
8.2
to obtain the limits. The contours
C(\delta;\pi/3)
satisfy
\max\{0,\tilde{\beta}\}<\delta<\tilde{t}
.
∎

Lemma 12.3
.

Fix any
\max\{\tilde{\beta},0\}<\delta<\tilde{t}
and any
s\in\mathbb{R}.
There exists
N_{0}\in\Z_{>0}
and
C
depending on
N_{0},\delta
but independent of
u,s
such that for all
u\geq s,
and
N\geq N_{0}
, we have the following upper bounds:

(12.5)

\displaystyle|\widehat{\aleph}_{1}(s,u)|,|(\sigma N)^{1/3}\widehat{\aleph}_{2}(s,u)|\leq Ce^{-2\delta s}e^{-u\delta},\quad|(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\widehat{\psi}_{1}(s,u)|,|\widehat{\psi}_{2}(s,u)|\leq Ce^{s(\tilde{t}-\delta)}e^{-u\delta},

\displaystyle|\widehat{\Theta}_{1}(s,u)|,|(\sigma N)^{1/3}\widehat{\Theta}_{2}(s,u)|\leq Ce^{-2\delta s}e^{-u\delta},\quad|\widehat{\zeta}_{1}(s,v)|,|(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\widehat{\zeta}_{2}(s,v)|\leq Ce^{s(\tilde{t}-\delta)}e^{-v\delta}

\displaystyle|(\sigma N)^{1/3}\widehat{\eta}_{1}(s,u)|,|\widehat{\eta}_{2}(s,u)|\leq Ce^{-s(\tilde{\beta}+\delta)}e^{-v\delta},\quad|(\sigma N)^{1/3}\widehat{\theta}_{1}(s,v)|,|\widehat{\theta}_{2}(s,v)|\leq Ce^{-2s\delta}e^{-v\delta}.

Proof.

All upper bounds can be proved in the same manner. We illustrate the argument using
\widehat{\psi}_{1}
as an example. We define
\text{Int}(\widehat{\psi}_{1})=\frac{(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})(\tilde{Z}+\tilde{t})(\tilde{\beta}+\tilde{W})(\tilde{\beta}+\tilde{Z})(\tilde{Z}-\tilde{W})}{8\tilde{t}\tilde{Z}\tilde{W}(\tilde{\beta}+\tilde{t})(\tilde{Z}-\tilde{t})(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{W})(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{Z})(\tilde{Z}+\tilde{W})}.
Applying the parameter scaling and the change of variables in (
8.3
) to
\widehat{\psi}_{1}
yields

(12.6)

\displaystyle|(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\widehat{\psi}_{1}(s,u)|=e^{s(\tilde{t}-\delta)-u\delta}\mathcal{O}(e^{-\epsilon N})

\displaystyle+\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{C(\delta;\pi/3)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{d\tilde{W}}{2\pi{\rm i}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{C(\delta;\pi/3)}\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{d\tilde{Z}}{2\pi{\rm i}}e^{\frac{\tilde{Z}^{3}}{3}-s\tilde{Z}-\frac{\tilde{t}^{3}}{3}+s\tilde{t}+\frac{\tilde{W}^{3}}{3}-u\tilde{W}+\mathcal{O}((\sigma N)^{-1/3})}\bigg|\text{Int}(\widehat{\psi}_{1})+\mathcal{O}((\sigma N)^{-1/3})\bigg|\leq Ce^{s(\tilde{t}-\delta)-u\delta}.

Here, the term
\mathcal{O}(e^{-\epsilon N})
arises from integration over portions of the contour away from the critical point. The final bound follows since the factor
e^{\frac{\tilde{Z}^{3}}{3}+\frac{\tilde{W}^{3}}{3}}
decays exponentially along the contours, yielding a finite constant
C>0.

∎

Lemma 12.4
.

Fix any
\max\{\tilde{\beta},0\}<\delta<\tilde{t}
and any
s\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\tau=-Nf+(\sigma N)^{1/3}s
. We have the following limit:

(12.7)

\begin{split}&\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Pf}\left(J-\bm{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}-\widehat{A}\right)=\mathrm{Pf}\left(J-\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}-\widetilde{A}\right).\end{split}

where
\widehat{A}
is one of the following matrix kernels:

(12.8)

\displaystyle(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\zeta}_{2}\\
-\widehat{\zeta}_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\widehat{\aleph}_{1}\quad\widehat{\aleph}_{2}\right|+(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\aleph}_{1}\\
\widehat{\aleph}_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\widehat{\zeta}_{2}\quad\widehat{\zeta}_{1}\right|,

\displaystyle\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\eta}_{2}\\
-\widehat{\eta}_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\widehat{\Theta}_{1}\quad\widehat{\Theta}_{2}\right|+\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\Theta}_{1}\\
\widehat{\Theta}_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\widehat{\eta}_{2}\quad\widehat{\eta}_{1}\right|,

\displaystyle\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\eta}_{2}\\
-\widehat{\eta}_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\widehat{\aleph}_{1}\quad\widehat{\aleph}_{2}\right|+\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\aleph}_{1}\\
\widehat{\aleph}_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\widehat{\eta}_{2}\quad\widehat{\eta}_{1}\right|,

\displaystyle\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\theta}_{2}\\
-\widehat{\theta}_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\widehat{\aleph}_{1}\quad\widehat{\aleph}_{2}\right|+\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\aleph}_{1}\\
\widehat{\aleph}_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\widehat{\theta}_{2}\quad\widehat{\theta}_{1}\right|,

\displaystyle(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\eta}_{2}\\
-\widehat{\eta}_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\widehat{\psi}_{1}\quad\widehat{\psi}_{2}\right|+(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\psi}_{1}\\
\widehat{\psi}_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\widehat{\eta}_{2}\quad\widehat{\eta}_{1}\right|,

\displaystyle(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\theta}_{2}\\
-\widehat{\theta}_{1}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle\widehat{\psi}_{1}\quad\widehat{\psi}_{2}\right|+(\sigma N)^{-1/3}\left|\begin{array}[]{c}\widehat{\psi}_{1}\\
\widehat{\psi}_{2}\end{array}\right\rangle\left\langle-\widehat{\theta}_{2}\quad\widehat{\theta}_{1}\right|,

and
\widetilde{A}
denotes the corresponding limit under the critical scaling. In particular, for
i\in\{1,2\},
the functions
\widetilde{\psi_{i}},\widetilde{\zeta_{i}},\widetilde{\aleph_{i}},\widetilde{\theta_{i}},\widetilde{\eta_{i}},\widetilde{\Theta_{i}}
denote the limits of
\widehat{\psi_{i}},\widehat{\zeta_{i}},\widehat{\aleph_{i}},\widehat{\theta_{i}},\widehat{\eta_{i}},\widehat{\Theta_{i}}
respectively, after applying the appropriate
(\sigma N)^{1/3}
scaling specified in (
12.5
).

Proof.

Since we fix
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we only use the upper bounds in terms of
u
from Lemma
12.3
, treating the dependence on
s
as a constant. By Lemma
12.3
and Lemma
12.1
, we get that for all choices of
\widehat{A}
and for all
u,v\geq s
, there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
u,v
such that

(12.9)

\bigg|\bm{\widehat{\mathcal{K}}}+\widehat{A}\bigg|(u,v)\leq\begin{pmatrix}Ce^{-\delta(u+v)}&Ce^{-\delta(u+v)}\\
Ce^{-\delta(u+v)}&Ce^{-\delta(u+v)}\end{pmatrix}.

The convergence of the Fredholm Pfaffian then follows from the dominated convergence theorem together with Hadamard’s bound.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.14
.

Theorem
1.14
follows from Lemmas
12.1
,
12.2
, and
12.4
provided that
\Pf\left(J-\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}\right)\neq 0
. Since the point-wise limit of increasing function is still increasing,
\mathcal{J}(s)=\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\widetilde{\NN}-\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}{\Pf\left(J-\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}\right)}
is increasing in
s
. Due to the same reason as in the proof of Theorem
1.13
, we see that all zeros of
\Pf\left(J-\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{K}}}\right)
is a removable singularity and the equality in (
1.20
) can be extended to
\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.15
.

Fix any
\tilde{\beta}
,
\tilde{t}
with
-\tilde{t}<\tilde{\beta}<\tilde{t}.

By the tightness proved in Theorem
13.6
and Theorem
14.5
, we know that there exists a limiting random variable
\mathcal{X}_{\tilde{\beta}}
such that
\frac{\log Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)+Nf}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}
converges weakly along a subsequence. Define
F_{\beta,N}(r)=\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\log Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)+Nf}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}\leq r\right)
and
\widetilde{F}_{\tilde{\beta}}(r)=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\tilde{\beta}}\leq r).
By Theorem
13.6
, we have that for any
\epsilon>0,
there exists
C>0
,
N_{0}\in\Z_{\geq 0}
such that for all
N\geq N_{0},

{F}_{\beta,N}(r)\leq Ce^{(1-\epsilon)2\tilde{t}|r|}
for all
r<0
. We thus have the same lower tail upper bound for
\widetilde{F}_{\tilde{\beta}}
.
We use similar arguments as in the proof of Theorem
1.8
. By Corollary
15.2
, we have for any
1\gg\epsilon>0
, there exists
N_{0}>0
,
C>0
such that for all
N\geq N_{0},

(12.10)

|(\sigma N)^{-1/3}Q_{N,\beta<t}^{High}(-2N\psi(\alpha)+x(\sigma N)^{1/3})|\leq C|x|e^{-(1-\epsilon)2\tilde{t}|x|}\quad\text{ for all }x<0.

The difference in this case is the upper tail of
{Q}_{N,\beta<t}^{High}
. By Lemma
16.7
, there exists
C>0,

s_{0}>0
and
N_{0}
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
and all
x>s_{0}
,

|(\sigma N)^{-1/3}Q_{N,\beta<t}^{High}(-2N\psi(\alpha)+x(\sigma N)^{1/3})|\leq Ce^{x(\tilde{t}-\tilde{\beta})}.

Fix any
\epsilon\ll 1
such that
(1-\epsilon)2\tilde{t}>\tilde{t}-\tilde{\beta}.
We need to choose
A
such that
(1-\epsilon)2\tilde{t}>A>\tilde{t}-\tilde{\beta}>0
. Then by dominated convergence theorem, we obtain the limit

(12.11)

\displaystyle\frac{(A-{\rm i}y)}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{Q}_{\tilde{\beta}<\tilde{t}}^{High}(x+r)e^{-Ax}e^{{\rm i}xy}dx=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\widetilde{F}_{\tilde{\beta}}(x+r-u)(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})e^{-(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})u}e^{-Ax}e^{{\rm i}xy}dudx.

We verify that such Fourier transform is invertible by showing that

H(z):=e^{-Az}\int_{0}^{\infty}\widetilde{F}_{\tilde{\beta}}(z+r-u)\,e^{-u(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})}\,du

belongs to
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\lambda=(1-\epsilon)2\tilde{t}
. By Theorem
13.6
, we have
|\widetilde{F}_{\tilde{\beta}}(z)|\leq Ce^{\lambda z}
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
. For
z<0
, recall that we have chosen
\lambda>A>\tilde{t}-\tilde{\beta}
,

(12.12)

|H(z)|\leq e^{-Az}\int_{0}^{\infty}Ce^{\lambda(z+r-u)-u(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})}du=C^{\prime}e^{(\lambda-A)z}.

For
z>0,

(12.13)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\widetilde{F}_{\tilde{\beta}}(z+r-u)\,e^{-u(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})}\,du\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{z+r}e^{-u(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})}du+\int_{z+r}^{\infty}Ce^{\lambda(z+r-u)}e^{-u(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})}du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tilde{t}-\tilde{\beta}}\left(e^{-(z+r)(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})}-1\right)+e^{\lambda(z+r)}\int_{z+r}^{\infty}Ce^{-(\lambda+\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})u}du

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\left(e^{-(z+r)(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})}+e^{\lambda(z+r)}e^{-(\lambda+\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})(z+r)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime}e^{(\tilde{t}-\tilde{\beta})z}

Since
A>\tilde{t}-\tilde{\beta}
, we have
H(z)\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}).
Then we can apply the fourier inversion to (
12.11
) and get

(12.14)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\widetilde{F}_{\tilde{\beta}}(r-u)(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})e^{-(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})u}du=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(A-{\rm i}y)\widetilde{Q}_{\tilde{\beta}<\tilde{t}}^{High}(x+r)e^{-Ax}e^{{\rm i}xy}dxdy.

Since we know the above equality holds for all
r
,
\widetilde{F}_{\tilde{\beta}}
can be uniquely characterized. Hence, the full sequence converges to
\mathcal{X}_{\tilde{\beta}}
. Now, we write
F_{\beta,N}(x)=F_{x,N}(\tilde{\beta})
to emphasize the dependence on
\tilde{\beta}
for fixed
x
. Assume
\tilde{\beta}>\tilde{t}
. We have
(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})e^{-(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})u}
is the density function of Exp
(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})
.
By the deconvolution method in
[
11
, Lemma 3.3]
, we get the following identity for any fixed
r
,
N
,
\tilde{\beta}
,
\tilde{t}
,

(12.15)

{F}_{r,N}(\tilde{\beta})=\left(1+\frac{\partial_{r}}{\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t}}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}{F}_{r-u,N}(\tilde{\beta})(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})e^{-(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})u}du.

We have that
F_{x,N}(\tilde{\beta})
is an analytic function of
\tilde{\beta}
because density function of Gamma inverse random variable is an analytic function in its parameters and this integral is well-defined under
\tilde{\beta}\in(-\tilde{t},\tilde{t})
. Then we can analytically extend (
12.15
) to
\tilde{\beta}\in(-\tilde{t}+\varsigma,\tilde{t}-\varsigma)
for some
0<\varsigma\ll 1
. Finally, taking the limit
N\rightarrow\infty
of (
12.15
) gives

(12.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{X}_{\tilde{\beta}}\leq r\right)=\widetilde{F}_{\tilde{\beta}}(r)=\left(1+\frac{\partial_{r}}{\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t}}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\widetilde{F}_{\tilde{\beta}}(r-u)(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})e^{-(\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t})u}du

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{\partial_{r}}{\tilde{\beta}-\tilde{t}}\right)\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(A-{\rm i}y)\widetilde{Q}_{\tilde{\beta}<\tilde{t}}^{High}(x+r)e^{-Ax}e^{{\rm i}xy}dxdy.

∎

13.
Lower tail estimates for half-space stationary models

In this section, we prove lower tail estimates for the half-space stationary models using three main ingredients: stochastic dominance via coupling, the distribution correspondence between full-space and half-space log-gamma partition functions, and random walk estimates. We first introduce these tools.

13.1.
Stochastic dominance via coupling

Lemma 13.1

(Stochastic dominance with respect to shape parameters)
.

Let
D\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be a connected set. Let
\{\alpha_{v}\}_{v\in D}
and
\{\tilde{\alpha}_{v}\}_{v\in D}
be two sets of shape parameters such that

(13.1)

\alpha_{v}\geq\tilde{\alpha}_{v}\qquad\text{for all }v\in D.

For each
v\in D
, let

(13.2)

W_{v}\sim\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha_{v}),\qquad\widetilde{W}_{v}\sim\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\tilde{\alpha}_{v}),

be independent collections of inverse-gamma random variables with shape parameters

\{\alpha_{v}\}_{v\in D}
and
\{\tilde{\alpha}_{v}\}_{v\in D}
, respectively. Let
\Pi_{x\to y}(D)
denote the set of up-right paths in
D
from
x
to
y
, and define the
corresponding partition functions

(13.3)

Z(x,y):=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{x\to y}(D)}\prod_{v\in\pi}W_{v},\qquad\widetilde{Z}(x,y):=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{x\to y}(D)}\prod_{v\in\pi}\widetilde{W}_{v}.

Then, there exists a coupling of
Z
and
\widetilde{Z}
such that

\log Z(x,y)\leq\log\widetilde{Z}(x,y).

We write it as
\log Z(x,y)\leq_{\mathrm{st}}\log\widetilde{Z}(x,y).

Proof.

Recall that the inverse-gamma random variable with parameter
\alpha
has the following cumulative distribution function

(13.4)

F(\alpha;x)=\int_{0}^{x}\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha)}x^{-\alpha-1}e^{-x^{-1}}dx.

Let
\rho_{\alpha}(x)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha)}x^{-\alpha-1}e^{-x^{-1}}
. Let
H(\alpha;\cdot)
be the inverse of
F(\alpha;\cdot)
defined on the interval
(0,1)
. We can calculate the first logarithmic derivative of
H
with respect to
\alpha
:

(13.5)

\partial_{\alpha}\log H(\alpha;x)=-\frac{1}{y\rho_{\alpha}(y)}\mathrm{Cov}(-\log X,\mathbbm{1}[X\leq y])<0

where
y=H(\alpha;x)
and
X\sim\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha)
.

Now let
\{U_{v}\}_{v\in D}
be a sequence of independent uniform
[0,1]
random variables. Setting

(13.6)

W_{v}=H(\alpha_{v};U_{v}),\quad\widetilde{W}_{v}=H(\tilde{\alpha}_{v};U_{v})

yields a coupling under which

(13.7)

W_{v}\sim\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha_{v}),\qquad\widetilde{W}_{v}\sim\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\tilde{\alpha}_{v}).

Since
\alpha_{v}\geq\tilde{\alpha}_{v}
for all
v\in D
and
\partial_{\alpha}\log H(\alpha,x)<0
for all
\alpha>0
, we have

(13.8)

W_{v}\leq\widetilde{W}_{v}

for all
v\in D
and thus

(13.9)

\log Z(x,y)\leq\log\widetilde{Z}(x,y).

∎

13.2.
Correspondence between the full-space and half-space log-gamma partition function

The correspondence is encoded in the following theorem
[
9
, Theorem 1.4]
:

Theorem 13.2
.

Fix integers
n\geq 1
and
m\geq 0
. Let
\alpha^{\circ}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\beta=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}

satisfy

(13.10)

\alpha_{i}+\alpha^{\circ}>0,\qquad\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}>0,\qquad\alpha_{i}+\beta_{k}>0.

for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

(A) Full-space point-to-point polymer.

Consider the rectangular domain

(13.11)

\{(i,j):1\leq i\leq n+m+1,\ 1\leq j\leq n\}.

Let the weights on the rectangular domain be independently distributed as follows:

(13.12)

W_{i,j}\sim\begin{cases}\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha^{\circ}+\alpha_{j}),&i=1,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha_{i-1}+\alpha_{j}),&2\leq i\leq n+1,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\beta_{i-n-1}+\alpha_{j}),&n+2\leq i\leq n+m+1.\end{cases}

Let
Z_{full}(n+m+1,n)
denote the full-space log-gamma polymer partition function from
(1,1)
to
(n+m+1,n)
according to the model above.

(B) Trapezoidal point-to-line polymer.

Consider the trapezoidal domain

(13.13)

\bigl\{(i,j):1\leq j\leq n,\ \ j\leq i\leq 2n+m-j+1\bigr\}.

Let the weights on the trapezoid domain be independently distributed as follows:

(13.14)

W_{i,j}\sim\begin{cases}\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha_{i}+\alpha^{\circ}),&1\leq i=j\leq n,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha_{j}+\alpha_{i}),&1\leq j<i\leq n,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha_{j}+\beta_{i-n}),&1\leq j\leq n,\ \ n<i\leq n+m,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha_{j}+\alpha_{2n+m-i+1}),&1\leq j\leq n,\ \ n+m<i\leq 2n+m-j+1.\end{cases}

Let
Z_{trap}(n,m)
denote the log-gamma polymer partition function from
(1,1)
to
(n,m)
according to the model above. Let
Z_{line}
be the trapezoidal point-to-line partition defined as follows:

(13.15)

Z_{line}(n+m+1,n):=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}Z_{trap}(n+m+k+1,n-k).

Then the following identity in distribution holds:

(13.16)

Z_{full}(n+m+1,n)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\ Z_{line}(n+m+1,n).

Additionally, we need the following lower tail result from the full space model
[
10
, Proposition 3.8]
.

Theorem 13.3
.

Consider the homogeneous full-space model as shown in Figure
7
where the weight at
(1,1)
is
1
and the rest of the weights are distributed as independent
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(2\alpha)
. Let
\tilde{Z}_{full}(N,N)
denote the point-to-point partition function from
(1,1)
to
(N,N)
of this model. Then there exist constants
c
,
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
and
t\geq 1
, we have

(13.17)

\mathbb{P}\left(\log\tilde{Z}_{full}(N+1,N+1)+2N\psi(\alpha)\leq-tN^{1/3}\right)\leq e^{-c\min\{t^{3/2},tN^{1/3}\}}.

13.3.
Random Walk Estimates

Lemma 13.4
.

Let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{N}\sim\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha+t)
and
Y_{1},\cdots,Y_{N}\sim\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha-t)
be two sequences of independent random variables. Let
W_{1}=\log X_{1}
,
W_{i}=\log X_{i}-\log Y_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq N
. We define the sum
S_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}W_{i}
for
1\leq k\leq N
. Take
t={\tilde{t}}/{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}
for some
\tilde{t}>0
. There exist a positive constant
C
and a positive integer
N_{0}
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
and
a>0
, we have

(13.18)

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{1\leq k\leq N}S_{k}>a(\sigma N)^{1/3}\right)\leq Ce^{-2\tilde{t}a}.

Proof.

We define the exponential martingale for
1\leq k\leq N
as the following

(13.19)

M_{k}=\exp\left(\lambda S_{k}-\Lambda_{k}\right)

where

(13.20)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{k}:=

\displaystyle k\log\mathbbm{E}[\exp(\lambda\log X_{1})]+(k-1)\log\mathbbm{E}[\exp(-\lambda Y_{1})]

and for
\alpha\pm(t-\lambda)>0
,

(13.21)

\begin{split}&\log\mathbbm{E}[\exp(\lambda\log X_{1})]=\log\Gamma(\alpha+t-\lambda)-\log\Gamma(\alpha+t)\\
&\log\mathbbm{E}[\exp(-\lambda\log Y_{1})]=\log\Gamma(\alpha-t+\lambda)-\log\Gamma(\alpha-t).\end{split}

We want to apply Doob’s maximal inequality to the exponential martingale:

(13.22)

\PP\left(\max_{1\leq k\leq N}M_{k}\geq K\right)\leq\mathbbm{E}[M_{N}]K^{-1}=K^{-1}.

To do so, we need to upper bound
\Lambda_{k}
for all
1\leq k\leq N
. Let
\lambda=\frac{\tilde{\lambda}}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}
for some
\tilde{\lambda}
to be chosen later. By taylor expansion,

(13.23)

\log\mathbbm{E}[\exp(\lambda W_{1})]=\frac{(\tilde{t}-\tilde{\lambda})^{2}-\tilde{t}^{2}}{(\sigma N)^{2/3}}\psi^{(1)}(\alpha)+O(N^{-4/3}).

Because
\psi^{(1)}(\alpha)>0
, as long as
(\tilde{t}-\tilde{\lambda})^{2}-\tilde{t}^{2}\leq 0
, we know that there exists some positive constant
C
such that for all positive integer
N
this holds:
\max_{1\leq k\leq N}\Lambda_{k}<C
. Thus, we choose
\tilde{\lambda}=2\tilde{t}
and get that

(13.24)

\displaystyle\PP\left(\max_{1\leq k\leq N}S_{k}>a(\sigma N)^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\PP\left(\max_{1\leq k\leq N}M_{k}>\exp\left(\lambda a(\sigma N)^{1/3}-C\right)\right)\leq e^{C-2\tilde{t}a}.

∎

13.4.
Proof of lower tail for half-space product stationary model

\footnotesize{1}
{\widetilde{Z}_{full}(N+1,N)}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}

Figure 7
.
Homogeneous full space polymer model

\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{1}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{\widetilde{Z}_{line}(N+1,N)}

Figure 8
.
Trapozoid homogeneous log-gamma polymer

\footnotesize{\alpha-t}
\footnotesize{\alpha-t}
\footnotesize{\alpha-t}
\footnotesize{\alpha-t}
\footnotesize{1}
\footnotesize{Z^{t}(N,N)}
\footnotesize{Z^{t}(N+1,N)}
\footnotesize{\alpha-t}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{\alpha+t}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{2\alpha}
\footnotesize{Z^{t}_{line}(N+1,N)}

Figure 9
.
Trapozoid product stationary log-gamma polymer

Theorem 13.5
.

Let
\log Z^{t}(N,N)
be the free energy of the half-space model defined in Definition
4.3
. Let us scale
t=\frac{\tilde{t}}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}
for some fixed
\tilde{t}>0
. For any small
\varepsilon>0
, there exist positive constants
C
and
N_{0}
such that for all
N\geq N_{0},
and
x>0
, we have

(13.25)

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\log Z^{t}(N,N)+2N\psi(\alpha)}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}\leq-x\right)\leq Ce^{-(1-\varepsilon)2\tilde{t}x}.

Proof.

Let
Z_{full}(N+1,N)
and
Z_{line}(N+1,N)
denote the full-space point-to-point partition function and trapezoidal point-to-line partition function defined in Theorem
13.2
with
\alpha^{\circ}=\alpha_{1}=\cdots=\alpha_{N}=\alpha
. Moreover, let
\widetilde{Z}_{full}(N+1,N)
and
\widetilde{Z}_{line}(N+1,N)
denote the same partition functions except the weights at
(1,1)
in both models are replaced by
1
, as shown in Figure
7
and Figure
8
respectively. We know from Theorem
13.2
that

(13.26)

Z_{full}(N+1,N)\overset{d}{=}Z_{line}(N+1,N).

For both models, the weights at
(1,1)
are independent from the rest and have the same
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(2\alpha)
distribution. Since the characteristic function for log-inverse-gamma random variable with parameter
2\alpha
is never zero, we can conclude

(13.27)

\widetilde{Z}_{full}(N+1,N)\overset{d}{=}\widetilde{Z}_{line}(N+1,N).

On the same trapezoidal domain

(13.28)

\{(i,j):1\leq j\leq N,j\leq i\leq 2N+1-j\}

we define the following independently distributed weights as shown in Figure
9

(13.29)

W^{t}_{i,j}\sim\begin{cases}1,&i=j=1,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha-t),&2\leq i=j\leq N,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha+t),&j=1,2\leq i\leq 2N,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(2\alpha),&j\geq 2,j<i\leq 2N+1-j\end{cases}

Let
Z^{t}(N+k,N+1-k)
denote the point-to-point partition function from
(1,1)
to
(N+k,N+1-k)
for the model defined above. Moreover, let

(13.30)

Z^{t}_{line}(N+1,N)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}Z^{t}(N+k,N+1-k).

By Lemma
13.1
, since
\alpha-t,\alpha+t\leq 2\alpha
for large enough
N
, for any
\varsigma\in\mathbb{R}
and large enough
N

(13.31)

\PP\left(Z^{t}_{line}(N+1,N)\leq\varsigma\right)\leq\PP\left(\tilde{Z}_{line}(N+1,N)\leq\varsigma\right)=\PP\left(\tilde{Z}_{full}(N+1,N)\leq\varsigma\right).

Let
T_{k}=\log Z^{t}(N+k,N+1-k)
for
1\leq k\leq N
. By the stationarity of the model (
14.9
),
T_{k}-\log Z^{t}(N,N)
has the same distribution as
S_{k}
, defined in Lemma
13.4
. Thus, apply Lemma
13.4
, we have

(13.32)

\PP\left(\max_{1\leq k\leq N}T_{k}-\log Z^{t}(N,N)\geq a(\sigma N)^{1/3}\right)\leq Ce^{-2\tilde{t}a}.

Because

(13.33)

\log Z^{t}_{line}(N+1,N)\leq\log N+\max_{1\leq k\leq N}T_{k},

we have for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)

(13.34)

\displaystyle\PP\bigg(\log Z^{t}(N,N)+2N\psi(\alpha)

\displaystyle\leq-a(\sigma N)^{1/3}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\PP\left(\log Z^{t}(N,N)-\log{Z}^{t}_{line}(N+1,N)\leq-(1-\varepsilon)a(\sigma N)^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle+\PP\left(\log{Z}^{t}_{line}(N+1,N)+2N\psi(\alpha)\leq-\varepsilon a(\sigma N)^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\PP\left(\max_{1\leq k\leq N}T_{k}-\log Z^{t}(N,N)\geq(1-\varepsilon)a(\sigma N)^{1/3}-\log N\right)

\displaystyle+\PP\left(\log\tilde{Z}_{full}(N+1,N)+2N\psi(\alpha)\leq-\varepsilon a(\sigma N)^{1/3}\right)

Because of the full-space lower tail (
13.17
) and the random walk estimates (
13.32
), we know that there exist positive constants
C,N_{0}
such that the probability above is bounded by
Ce^{-(1-\varepsilon)2\tilde{t}a}
for all
N\geq N_{0}.

∎

13.5.
Proof of lower tail for half-space two-parameter stationary model

Theorem 13.6
.

Let
\log Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)
be the free energy of the half-space model defined in Definition
4.4
and shown in Figure
11
. Let us scale
t=\frac{\tilde{t}}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}},\beta=\frac{\tilde{\beta}}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}
for some fixed
\tilde{\beta}>-\tilde{t}
. For any small
\varepsilon>0
, there exist positive constants
C
and
N_{0}
such that for all
N\geq N_{0},
and
x>0
, we have

(13.35)

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\log Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)+2N\psi(\alpha)}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}\leq-x\right)\leq Ce^{-(1-\varepsilon)2\tilde{t}x}.

1

\beta+t
\alpha+t
\alpha+t
\alpha+t
\alpha+t
Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)
\alpha-t
\alpha+\beta
2\alpha

2\alpha

Figure 10
.
Two parameter stationary
Z^{t,\beta}

1
1
\alpha+t
\alpha+t
\alpha+t

\alpha+t
\overline{Z}^{t}(N,N)
\alpha-t
2\alpha
2\alpha
2\alpha
2\alpha

Figure 11
.
Product stationary with the weight at
(2,1)
replaced by
1
:
\overline{Z}^{t}
.

Proof.

Let us start with the modified half-space product stationary model as illustrated in Figure
11
where the
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha-t)
weight at
(2,1)
in the product-stationary model is replaced by constant
1
. Let
\overline{Z}^{t}
denote the partition function of this model and
Z^{t}
denote the partition function of the product-stationary model. Thus,
\log\overline{Z}^{t}(N,N)+\log G\overset{d}{=}\log Z^{t}(N,N)
for
G\sim\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha-t)
. For
x>0
, we have for any
\delta\in(0,1)

(13.36)

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\log\overline{Z}^{t}(N,N)+2N\psi(\alpha)}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}\leq-x\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\log{Z}^{t}(N,N)+2N\psi(\alpha)}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}\leq-(1-\delta)x\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\log G}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}\geq\delta x\right).

By Theorem
13.5
, the first term is bounded by
Ce^{-(1-\varepsilon)(1-\delta)2\tilde{t}x}
for all large enough
N
. For the second term, we know it is bounded by

(13.37)

\mathbb{P}\left(\log G^{\prime}\geq(\sigma N)^{1/3}\delta x\right)

for
G^{\prime}\sim\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha/2)
for
N
large enough by Lemma
13.1
. Since
\log\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha/2)
is a sub-exponential random variable
[
10
, Proposition D.2]
, the second term is thus bounded by

C^{\prime}e^{-c^{\prime}(\sigma N)^{1/3}\delta x}

for some
c^{\prime},C^{\prime}>0
. Combining these two bounds, we get that for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exist positive constants
C
and
N_{0}
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
and
x>0
,

(13.38)

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\log\overline{Z}^{t}(N,N)+2N\psi(\alpha)}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}\leq-x\right)\leq Ce^{-(1-\varepsilon)2\tilde{t}x}.

Again by Lemma
13.1
, we see that
\log\overline{Z}^{t}(N,N)\leq_{\mathrm{st}}\log Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)
for large enough
N
and hence

(13.39)

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\log{Z}^{t,\beta}(N,N)+2N\psi(\alpha)}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}\leq-x\right)\leq\left(\frac{\log\overline{Z}^{t}(N,N)+2N\psi(\alpha)}{(\sigma N)^{1/3}}\leq-x\right)\leq Ce^{-(1-\varepsilon)2\tilde{t}x}.

∎

14.
Upper tail estimates for half-space stationary models

In this section, we derive upper tail estimates for the half-space stationary models. Our analysis combines analytic continuation and steepest descent analysis of the Fredholm determinant, together with the correspondence between full-space and half-space log-gamma partition functions.

We will first prove the upper tail estimates for the free energy
\log Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)
of the half-space two-parameter stationary model defined in Definition
4.4
. The upper tail estimates for the free energy
\log Z^{t}(N,N)
of the product stationary model defined in Definition
4.3
will follow as an easy consequence of Lemma
13.1
.

We start by decomposing the partition function
Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)
into two components as shown in Figure
12
. Let
\Pi_{x\rightarrow y}
denote the set of up-right paths in the half-space
\{(i,j)\in\Z^{2}:i\geq j\}
from
x
to
y
. Then,

(14.1)

Z^{t,\beta}(N,N)=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{(2,2)\rightarrow(N,N)}}\prod_{v\in\pi}w_{i,j}+\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{(3,1)\rightarrow(N,N)}}\prod_{v\in\pi}w_{i,j}

where
w_{i,j}
is the vertex weight defined according to Definition
4.4
.

1

\alpha+t

Z(N,N)

Figure 12
.
This is the half-space log-gamma polymer model with two parameter stationary initial condition specified in Definition
4.5
with
\gamma=1
. The black triangle contains all the paths in the first term of (
14.1
) and the blue trapezoid contains all the paths in the second term of (
14.1
).

We want to analyze these two terms separately. To this end, we introduce two auxiliary models whose observables reproduce the distributions of the respective terms.

(A)

Consider the trapezoidal domain

(14.2)

D_{N}=\{(i,j):1\leq j\leq N-2\text{ and }j\leq i\leq 2N-1-j\}.

Let the weights on the trapezoid domain be independently distributed as follows:

(14.3)

W_{i,j}\sim\begin{cases}\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha+t),&1\leq i=j\leq N-2,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha+\beta),&i=N-1,\ \ 1\leq j\leq N-2,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha-t),&i=N,\ \ 1\leq j\leq N-2,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(2\alpha),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Let
Z^{t,\beta}_{1}(n,m)
denote the log-gamma polymer partition function from
(1,1)
to
(n,m)
according to the model above with weights defined in Figure
14
. By symmetry, we see that

(14.4)

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{(3,1)\rightarrow(N,N)}}\prod_{v\in\pi}w_{i,j}\overset{d}{=}Z^{t,\beta}_{1}(N,N-2).

Let
Z^{t,\beta}_{1,line}
be the trapezoidal point-to-line partition defined as follows:

(14.5)

Z^{t,\beta}_{1,line}=\sum_{j=1}^{N-2}Z_{1}^{t,\beta}(2N-1-j,j).

Then, by the definition of partition function,

(14.6)

\log Z_{1}^{t,\beta}(N,N-2)+\log W_{N+1,N-2}\leq\log Z^{t,\beta}_{1}(N+1,N-2)\leq\log Z^{t,\beta}_{1,line}.

By Theorem
13.2
,

(14.7)

Z^{t,\beta}_{1,line}\overset{d}{=}Z^{t,\beta}_{1,full}(N+1,N-2)

where
Z^{t,\beta}_{1,full}(N+1,N-2)
is the full-space log-gamma polymer partition function from
(1,1)
to
(N+1,N-2)
with weights defined in Figure
14
.

(B)

Consider the trapezoidal domain

(14.8)

E_{N}=\{(i,j):1\leq j\leq N-1\text{ and }j\leq i\leq 2N-1-j\}.

Let the weights on the trapezoid domain be independently distributed as follows:

(14.9)

\widetilde{W}_{i,j}\sim\begin{cases}\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\beta+t),&i=j=1,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha+\beta),&2\leq i=j\leq N-1,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(\alpha+t),&2\leq i\leq 2N-3,\ \ j=1,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(2t),&i=2N-2,\ \ j=1,\\
\text{Gamma}^{-1}(2\alpha),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Let
Z^{t,\beta}_{2}(n,m)
denote the log-gamma polymer partition function from
(1,1)
to
(n,m)
according to the model above as shown in Figure
16
. By the symmetry property described in Lemma
4.2
, we can exchange the parameter
\beta
and
t
and get that

(14.10)

\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{(2,2)\rightarrow(N,N)}}\prod_{v\in\pi}w_{i,j}\overset{d}{=}Z^{t,\beta}_{2}(N-1,N-1).

Let
Z^{t,\beta}_{2,line}
be the trapezoidal point-to-line partition defined as follows:

(14.11)

Z^{t,\beta}_{2,line}=\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}Z_{2}^{t,\beta}(2N-1-j,j).

Then,

(14.12)

\log Z_{2}^{t,\beta}(N-1,N-1)+\log\widetilde{W}_{N,N-1}\leq\log Z^{t,\beta}_{2}(N,N-1)\leq Z^{t,\beta}_{2,line}.

Moreover, by Theorem
13.2
,

(14.13)

Z^{t,\beta}_{2,line}\overset{d}{=}Z^{t,\beta}_{2,full}(N,N-1)

where
Z^{t,\beta}_{2,full}(N,N-1)
is the full-space log-gamma polymer partition function from
(1,1)
to
(N,N-1)
with weights defined in Figure
16
.
